# `drag2` — probe 2: private kernel, private behavior runtime, contributed features

## Status

**Consolidated for an executable implementation probe.** This is the frozen construction model for `drag2`, revised after [review 1](../reviews/contract-probe-2/contract-probe-2-review-1.md) (answered in [`challenge-response.md`](challenge-response.md)), the React placeholder probe, [review 2](../reviews/contract-probe-2/contract-probe-2-review-2.md), [review 3](../reviews/contract-probe-2/contract-probe-2-review-3.md) and [review 4](../reviews/contract-probe-2/contract-probe-2-review-4.md), [review 5](../reviews/contract-probe-2/contract-probe-2-review-5.md) and [review 6](../reviews/contract-probe-2/contract-probe-2-review-6.md) — three pre-implementation consistency, correctness and cost passes.

**The signatures are compiled.** [`packages/drag/docs/contract-probe-2/contract.ts`](../../../../packages/drag/docs/contract-probe-2/contract.ts) holds the representative contracts, a reference sortable behavior implementing every seam, a driver for every seam (admission, activation, `moved`, action, release, settlement, arm, join), and `@ts-expect-error` assertions for **each tier-A claim that is expressible as a type error** — several, such as sealing and hold linearity, are sequencing rules no type can state. It is covered by `npx just typecheck` from `packages/drag`.

**It is a _type_ fixture, not an executable lifecycle reference.** It has no queue, no cancellation state machine, no failure checkpoint, no readiness watch and no landing completion dispatch; where a driver needs one it has an inert stub. At 2,300 lines it can look executable, so this is worth stating plainly: **typecheck cannot catch a lifecycle error there.** The executable cases live in the test matrix in [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md) and belong to the implementation. **These documents are the source of truth; where the fixture disagrees, the fixture is the bug.**

[`../contract/`](../contract/) — probe 1 — remains the reference for **lifecycle invariants, edge cases and validated product requirements**. Its _construction_ model (a public `Kernel` object, one shared runtime the behavior extends in place, a behavior that calls `begin()`/`commit()` itself, `Pick<>` projections over an aggregate runtime type) is not carried forward.

`challenge-response.md` is **frozen provenance**. Its arguments are folded in here; where it and these documents differ, **these documents win**. It is amended only for factual errata, never to carry a contract change — otherwise it drifts into being a second live version of the contract. Documents 00–06 are the only contract set.

### Normative precedence and freeze

The set is read with this precedence:

1. **00–04 are normative contracts**: decisions, ownership, lifecycle SPI, feature composition and frame rules.
2. In **05**, the invariant table, open questions, measurements owed and test matrix are normative. The chronological finding narratives are rationale; an older finding paragraph cannot override a later decision or the contracts in 00–04.
3. **07 is a normative behavior contract** — free drag's public surface and decomposition (Phase 18). It is subordinate to 00–04: where it and 02 disagree about a **seam**, 02 wins; where it and 03 disagree about **composition**, 03 wins. What is free-drag-specific is decided there and nowhere else.
4. **06 is an illustrative execution trace.** It must agree with 02, but never overrides 02 when wording drifts.
5. `contract.ts` is a type fixture only. Review files and `challenge-response.md` are provenance only.

The architecture decisions are frozen for the implementation probe. A further contract change requires a failing executable lifecycle case that the frozen SPI cannot express; a prose-only review finding is not sufficient. Performance and bundle choices explicitly listed as measurements remain open until measured.

**Revised once and re-frozen — Phase 14, 2026-08-04.** The rule above worked as designed: three probes ([`probes/`](../probes/README.md)) produced failing executable cases, and the contract was revised **once** against all three together rather than patched three times. The revision is **D-32…D-35**, and the reasoning for treating it as one change — including two decisions that would have been made differently in isolation — is §[02](02-kernel-behavior-contract.md) §Where the four changes touch each other. Nothing in the revision retracts a Part I result; what it changes is four places the SPI could not express something, three of which were the sortable's shape written into the kernel.

Re-freezing restores this rule unchanged. It does not close the contract: a later phase that produces a failing executable case reopens it, and that is the system working.

Checkpoint C reviewed the revision **five times, ran one follow-up round, and is closed. The SPI is re-frozen, and no decision was reopened after pass 1.** The first two passes changed D-33's mechanism (a kernel-minted token became controller acknowledgement by request identity) and the honesty of what I-35 claims. The third ([`checkpoint-c-3.md`](../reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-3.md)) changed **no** decision: it resolved two places where the same event had two normative readings, and made both report paths visible in the fixture.

The fourth ([`checkpoint-c-4.md`](../reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-4.md)) changed no decision either, and found two blockers anyway. Both are the same species: a decision that was right in the abstract and **unfinished in the seams that carry it.** D-32 said a command reuses the pointer lifecycle, and the normative sortable seams then overwrote the command's destination at activation and re-resolved it from a pointer sample at release — so the one thing D-32 puts in the draft was destroyed twice. D-35 said the lift session is the sole transform writer between acquisition and the join, which was wider than the property needed, false against this document's own landing contract, and rated a tier the API does not provide.

Both are corrected in place — §The command destination and §The landing origin — and neither reopened a decision. **The lesson is worth keeping: a revision is not finished when the SPI compiles.** Three passes of review looked at the four decisions and none of them looked at what the reference behavior had to do differently underneath them.

The correction pass then found two more of the same kind — `release.effect` had to branch as well, and the armed readiness window has an interior where two synchronous acknowledgements could each dispatch.

The fifth ([`checkpoint-c-5.md`](../reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-5.md)) found the same pattern **inside pass 4's own corrections**, and this is the part worth keeping. Pass 4 narrowed D-35's interval and downgraded its tier — correctly — and left the behavior holding `dispose()` on the kernel-owned lift session, so I-34 stayed breakable through a first-class method rather than through a documented residue. Pass 4 established that nothing may dispatch `READINESS_SETTLED` without first claiming the once-only latch — correctly — and applied it to the live armed path but not to arm's own dispatch of the copied early latch, so the race it closed in one window stayed open across the boundary to the other.

**Both corrections fixed the prose and left the type or the ordering.** That is a sharper lesson than "a revision is not finished when the SPI compiles": a correction is not finished when its _sentence_ is true. Where a rule names a capability, check the capability's type; where a rule names an order, check every site that performs it.

Three follow-ups (**C6-01…C6-03**, recorded in the contract without a separate review file) closed the checkpoint by applying that lesson to pass 5's own corrections: the projected `write` is retained and stays effective, so the blanket late-use-safe claim was narrowed and a **temporal** tier-C rule stated in its place — deliberately without a hot-path guard (§The temporal rule on `write`); publishing `rt.placeholder` and `rt.lift` created a clearing obligation and a failure path the fixture had not discharged; and one matrix row named a table position rather than the arrival rule. **Add a rule, and check what the rule now obliges.**

The re-freeze stands on that basis. What remains is not contract work: `src/` still implements the **pre-revision** SPI, so revised-runtime evidence belongs to Phases 15, 16 and 19–20, exactly as `plan.md` assigns it.

**Revised a second time — Revision 2, the API redesign, 2026-08-13.** Three API review rounds and five probes produced a redesign of the **consumer-facing** surface, recorded as **D-36…D-60** and owned by [`../reviews/api-reviews/api-review-final-summary.md`](../reviews/api-reviews/api-review-final-summary.md).

The freeze rule is discharged the same way Phase 14 discharged it, and it is worth being explicit about which decisions carry executable evidence and which do not, because the rule says a prose-only finding is not sufficient:

| Probe | Failing executable case | Decisions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **A** — transaction bracket ([`../probes/api-4-transaction-bracket.md`](../probes/api-4-transaction-bracket.md)) | 21 browser failures against the floor-preserving variant; 27 of 33 statement-level liveness readings retire; a manufactured `panic()` producer shows teardown leaving the throwing stack | D-36, D-37, D-38, D-39 |
| **C1** — the commit window ([`../probes/api-2-commit-window.md`](../probes/api-2-commit-window.md)) | 3 of 5 authored commits land at the viewport origin while reporting `onFinish` once and `onError` zero times; the placeholder runs 30 px too tall for a whole drag | D-42, D-43 |
| **E** — input policy ([`../probes/api-3-input-policy.md`](../probes/api-3-input-policy.md)) | 8 of 10 cases release-blocking; `ArrowRight` in a nested text input produces a complete accepted reorder from one keystroke | D-46 |
| **api-1** — layout ([`../probes/api-1-box-anchor.md`](../probes/api-1-box-anchor.md)) | measured: no single-window rule reproduces the removed footprint in both nested cases | D-43 |

**D-40, D-41, D-44, D-45, D-47, D-48, D-50, D-54, D-55, D-56, D-57 and D-58 carry no failing executable case, and are not claimed to.** They are owner decisions about the shape of a public API, or derivations forced by one, and the freeze rule does not govern them: it governs _the architecture decisions frozen for the implementation probe_ — the SPI. D-41 is the one that comes closest to the line, because it retracts D-33, which **was** a Phase-14 SPI revision; it is admitted here as a **deletion**, and a deletion needs no case that the SPI cannot express.

**Two decisions cross the frozen SPI, and neither must be buried.** **D-59** changes a seam signature — `admit`'s return widens to carry the box element — and is discharged by api-1 through the chain written in its row: no single-window footprint rule works, so D-43 needs a pre-lift box, and every carrier other than admission's return contradicts H-2. **D-53** adds a member to the frozen SPI — a logical-liveness reader on `KernelHost` — so the freeze rule governs it in full, and probe A discharges it: the three `presentation.signal.aborted` sites are the failing executable case, and they exist _precisely because_ the SPI could not express the reading D-38 requires. Every other row leaves the SPI alone. **D-49, D-51 and D-52 are repairs rather than additions** — each closes a hole that the other Revision 2 decisions opened in a rule that already existed, and each was found by a reviewer rather than authored here: D-51 in particular closes an exception D-37 could not survive without, since a finite domain with an unstated exception is not finite.

Revision 2 does not reopen Part I. What it changes is who the library is for: Phase 14 was four places the SPI could not express something, and this is the consumer surface that sits on top of it.

**Reconciled — Revision 2.1, 2026-08-13.** The owner read the reconciled contracts and found **five owner-decision drifts**: places where Revision 2 recorded a decision the owner had already made, or dropped one, and the contracts followed the record rather than the decision. They are **D-61…D-65**, and they are corrections to _this document's_ bookkeeping, not new architecture.

| # | Drift | Restores | Corrects |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-61 | The supported **middle tier** — sortable feature/config-utility authoring — was collapsed into the kernel tier | owner final §11 ladder; [`api-review-3-summary.md`](../reviews/api-reviews/api-review-3-summary.md) §1 | D-45's closing sentence, D-47's ladder, 03 §Fragments are public, installers are opaque |
| D-62 | The public terminal channel is **`onEnd`**; the contracts still carry `onFinish`/`onCancel` | owner final §4; review 3 §11 | D-40's own row, 02 §Callbacks and §Settlement, 03 §The schema |
| D-63 | The **public custom landing runner** was to be removed; `landing({ run })` survives | [`api-review-1-summary.md`](../reviews/api-reviews/api-review-1-summary.md) §5, review 2, review 3 §10 | D-56's surviving factory list, 03 §`landing()`, the preserved-from-probe-1 row |
| D-64 | **Coarse consumer error codes** with detailed stages internal; the 13 `FAILURE_*` constants are still ordinary-tier public | review 3 §12 | D-48's entry-topology _rationale_, 03 §The export topology, §The public/internal boundary |
| D-65 | **`placeholder`** is the consumer callback slot, not `createPlaceholder` + `placeholderClassName` | review 3 §2, §4 | D-56's flat-key split |

**The freeze rule does not govern any of the five, and none is claimed to carry a failing executable case.** Four restore standing owner decisions about a public API and one is a naming inconsistency; the SPI is untouched — D-53 and D-59 remain Revision 2's only two crossings. What D-63 removes from the _sortable_ surface it keeps at the _kernel_ tier, which review 3 §10 states explicitly, so no seam changes shape.

**Two further owner decisions closed what the reconciliation left open: D-66 resolves Q-14, and D-67 replaces the zero-argument landing thunk with a contextual `duration({ distance, from, to })`.** Both are recorded in the same table. D-66 is the one to read carefully — it retracts a rule 02 states normatively, and it narrows D-23.

**Three of the five cost something, and the cost is recorded in the row rather than absorbed:** D-61 puts the installer authoring types under a versioning promise, D-64 removes thirteen constants a consumer could switch on, and D-65 removes the class-only placeholder convenience D-56 argued for by name. **One reveals a contradiction that is not resolved here** — D-62 §The unresolved arm, below, and open question Q-14.

**The compiled fixture now exists** — [`tests/revision/revision-2.ts`](../../tests/revision/revision-2.ts), enforced by `npx just typecheck` — and it earned itself immediately. **Three of its sixteen assertions were false**, each a contract claim TypeScript does not actually enforce: F-51 (bivariant callback slots, repaired by named aliases), F-52 (the zero-argument thunk is not deletable), F-53 (`Promise<void>` flows into a `void` position). **These are the first defects in either revision found by a machine rather than by a reader**, and all three had survived a reconciliation, an owner pass and a review round in prose.

**One further claim was disproved by measurement rather than by the compiler**: F-54, the identity argument for keeping `drag.js`. See [`measurements/error-identity.md`](../measurements/error-identity.md).

**Revised a third time — Revision 2.2, the kernel vocabulary pass, 2026-08-14. One decision, D-68.** The fixture's rewiring to `src/` produced its second machine-found defect, and it is a **surface** defect rather than a type-level one: `kernel.js` publishes no value a behavior must produce, so the tier D-47 exists to publish **cannot be constructed from its own entry** — in any authoring style. See F-59. D-68 settles the vocabulary D-47 §11 queued and L-14 booked as owed, and it changes no seam, no signature and no entry count.

**What this episode is evidence of** is recorded rather than smoothed over: Revision 2 read the _final summary_ closely and the three review summaries underneath it loosely, and four of the five drifts are decisions that live only in the earlier rounds. The final summary's §12 asks to reconcile "against the decisions above" — and "above" was never the whole owner record. A reconciliation pass has to be run against the decision _history_, not against its most recent digest.

## The model

| # | Claim |
| --- | --- |
| H-1 | The kernel is an **internal executor**. It owns machine state, queue, frame pair, attempts, lifetimes, cancellation and teardown privately. It is not a public authoring object. |
| H-2 | A behavior is instantiated once per controller and owns a **completely private runtime** captured by its callbacks. The kernel does not know, store, extend or type it. |
| H-3 | The behavior supplies a **frame part** and a fixed set of **direct lifecycle callbacks**. The kernel selects transitions, opens and commits frames, and calls those callbacks. The behavior never calls `begin()` or `commit()`. |
| H-4 | A **feature is a function factory** that may create its own private runtime and returns a construction-time **contribution object** of named callbacks plus metadata. |
| H-5 | The behavior assembles contributions **once** into direct slots and fixed pipelines. No descriptor is interpreted on a runtime path. |
| H-6 | There is **one physical frame**, composed kernel-first from independently owned parts. **No participant declares a concrete whole-frame type.** (The kernel's own private generic _is_ `KernelFrame & Part`; the claim is about who authors a concrete shape, not about the absence of any intersection.) |

## Verdict

The model holds. Its load-bearing property is H-3: once the kernel owns `begin()`/`commit()`, the _prepare → commit → post-commit effect_ discipline stops being prose the behavior must obey and becomes the shape of the callback contract.

That guarantee must be stated precisely, in three tiers. Conflating them overstates what the API delivers.

| Tier | What it means | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **A — Frame publication safety** | The violation does not compile, or is unexpressible | `prepare` never receives `current`; `effect` receives `Readonly` (top-level slots); the behavior cannot write kernel frame fields; there is no behavior-callable `commit()`; a post-commit failure has no way to express a revert; release and settlement cannot discard |
| **B — Kernel-enforced sequencing** | The kernel orders it; the behavior has no opportunity to get it wrong | motion closes after the `RELEASING` commit and before the final measurement; revalidate-then-commit runs unconditionally; a settlement scope is sealed before any gate is armed; `dispose()` is projected off the lifetimes handed to the behavior; a seam throw is classified **and stops the seam's success continuation** |
| **C — Discipline** | A contract rule a participant must obey; the API permits violation | `prepare` performs no externally visible mutation; part factories are deterministic; `resetFramePart` is exhaustive; the shallow-copy contract and the immutability of frame _referents_; the ordering rule inside an `effect` |

Tier C is not a defect of this model — probe 1 had the same rules with none of A or B. But the honest claim is _"frame publication became structural"_, not _"preparation became safe"_. See [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md) for the per-invariant classification.

Concrete consequences of the model, none of which is offered as a headline metric:

- The kernel's frame slice is **seven fields it exclusively owns**. Settlement gates left the frame entirely for a kernel-private attempt.
- The behavior authors only its own frame part; it cannot mis-initialise kernel state because it cannot name it.
- Probe 1's open question **Q-5** (where the geometry cache lives) is answered by construction: inside the axis feature — `y()` or `xy()`, each holding its own instance of the shared `rect-index` cache. (Written as `vertical()` until Phase 17 renamed the axis features; see [03](03-feature-composition.md) §The export topology this requires.)
- Probe 1's pressure point **P-2** (a free drag must clamp before writing) is resolved at **no** hot-path cost — the move path spends the same three post-`MOVE` indirect calls probe 1 spent. That boundary is deliberate and narrow; the end-to-end count is M-1's job. **Tested by a second behavior at last** (13c P-1): the shape survives; the number is Phase 21's.
- Probe 1's **P-1** (no live policy seam) is resolved: policy updates are behavior actions and the kernel supplies the transition envelope. **Exercised** by 13c P-6 for the policy half. The _controlled position_ half was not resolved by it and is D-35's.

What this model does **not** improve: per-boundary revalidation inside a `prepare` (unchanged from probe 1 — one check, at the end), and the number of callbacks a behavior author implements (comparable to probe 1, not fewer).

**No hot-path or bundle number here is measured yet.** The four owed measurements are M-1…M-4 in [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md) §Measurements (landed 2026-08-02); until they land, the performance and tree-shaking statements in these documents are design intent, not evidence.

Inputs, not re-derivations.

| ~~Synchronous and terminal~~ **Immediate and terminal** `destroy()` | [01](01-construction-ownership.md) §Teardown. **Revision 2, D-36:** terminal is preserved and strengthened — the logical latch is set on the closing statement rather than at the end of a seven-step sequence. Synchronous is preserved only outside a reentrant transaction. |
| ~~Independent readiness and landing gates~~ **One landing gate** | [02](02-kernel-behavior-contract.md) §The settlement gate — requested, sealed, then armed, D-7. **Revision 2, D-41:** independence was the property that let readiness and landing overlap; with readiness deleted there is nothing to be independent of. Probe 1's underlying requirement — that absent landing costs no fake asynchronous work — is unaffected, since it was always the _default-open_ rule, not the gate count. |
| No fake asynchronous work when landing is absent | Gates default open; absence of a `landing()` feature holds **no landing gate**. Readiness is unaffected, and same-drain finalization requires neither gate held (I-9). |
| Layout displacement is not a lifecycle gate | [03](03-feature-composition.md) — a displacement hook cannot reach `SettlementScope` |

| Numeric spatial attempt identity where sufficient | [02](02-kernel-behavior-contract.md) §Attempts — behavior-private, D-11 |
| Correct cleanup during long-running landing | [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md) I-7 |
| Optional feature code remains tree-shakeable | [03](03-feature-composition.md) §Tree-shaking |
| Custom spring landing runner without a kernel change | [02](02-kernel-behavior-contract.md) §Landing — the kernel never names a runner type. **Revision 2.1, D-63: preserved at the kernel tier only.** The property was always about the _kernel_ not needing to change, and it is untouched; what is withdrawn is the **sortable's public `landing({ run })` slot**. A spring is still authorable by a kernel-tier behavior, and review 3 §10 says so explicitly. |

## Decision ledger

`D-n` cross-references probe 1's `C-n` where the two models disagree.

| ID | Decision | Why | vs probe 1 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-1 | `draggable(root, behavior)` uses a **two-phase handshake**: `behavior(host)` returns `{ spec, controller }`; the kernel then arms ingress. No `install()` side effect. | Makes "no input before install returns" unexpressible rather than a rule. | replaces C-1 |
| D-2 | The kernel is **not a value the behavior holds**. It hands out a small `KernelHost` at construction and per-seam arguments at call time. No exported `Kernel` or `KernelRuntime` type. | H-1. Six of probe 1's twelve `Kernel` members existed only because the behavior drove transitions. | replaces C-3 |
| D-3 | Every transactional seam is a **`Transition`**: `prepare(draft, capability) → Prepared \| null`, `effect(current, prepared, capability)`, optional `rollback(prepared)`. The kernel commits between prepare and effect and revalidates first. One shared **core returning a `SeamOutcome`**, and a **per-seam driver** that reads it: action discards normally, activation discard retires _but activation failure does not_, release and settlement cannot discard at all. | H-3. `Prepared` is the designated channel for staged state, so a discarded transition never has to have touched the private runtime. A `boolean` core conflated _discarded_ with _failed_, which let every seam continue success work after a classified failure (D-23). | new |
| D-4 | The behavior's runtime is **captured by closures**, not stored by the kernel. | H-2 verbatim. Alternative recorded as F-4. | replaces C-3 |
| D-5 | `admit(event, draft)` returns **the element to lift**, or `null`. | The kernel gets the one thing it needs from admission and nothing else. `item` is behavior state. | narrows C-4 |
| D-6 | Release is **two commits**: the kernel commits `RELEASING` and the release point, _then_ closes motion ingress, _then_ opens the transition in which the behavior resolves the final insertion and builds the proposal. | One extra frame copy per `pointerup`. Committing first means no irreversible physical action ever occurs while the committed frame describes a state that action has invalidated. | keeps C-4's ordering; the kernel now owns it |
| D-7 | Settlement gates default **open**, are **requested** during `settlement.effect`, and live on a **kernel-private settlement attempt** that is sealed when that callback returns and only then **armed**. Each may be requested at most once. **Narrowed to one gate by D-41 (Revision 2):** readiness is deleted, so the plan holds landing alone. Request-seal-arm survives unchanged — it is what makes a synchronous `done()` from a `duration: 0` runner safe, and that has nothing to do with readiness. | Encodes "no fake asynchronous work when landing is absent" as the default path, while keeping readiness independent of landing. Request-seal-arm is what makes a synchronous `done()` from a `duration: 0` runner safe. Gate state was never observable or transactional; putting it on the attempt makes staleness handling free. | replaces C-9's mechanism, keeps its semantics |
| D-8 | The **behavior** writes the lift transform, through `lift.write`. | Resolves P-2 at no cost: the same three post-`MOVE` indirect calls either way. **Extended by D-35 (Checkpoint C, C4-05):** `moved` is the hot path but is no longer the only site. A controlled position writes from an `action.effect` and the sortable's `release.effect` writes the final release sample — both through the same `lift.write`, which is why D-35 records inside the session rather than at one call site. Read "inside `moved`" as "through the session", or this row contradicts D-35. | replaces C-4 note |
| D-9 | ~~`Behavior<Controller, Part>`~~ — **superseded twice.** D-30 made the public value opaque `Behavior<Controller>`, and D-34 (Checkpoint C, C4-05) added `Activation`; **both `Part` and `Activation` are erased at the brand**. The three-parameter form survives on the **internal construction types** — `BehaviorFactory` and `BehaviorInstall` — neither of which a consumer names. (This said "only on `BehaviorFactory`" until Checkpoint C, C5-05; 01 defines both.) What this row still decides is the disjointness rule, unchanged. The kernel is internal, so a type parameter costs no public signature noise. **Disjointness from the kernel slice is enforced twice**: `FramePartOf<Part>` at the authoring boundary, `validateFramePart` in production at `arm()`. | Deletes probe 1's cast-at-the-boundary compromise. `Object.assign`'s `T & U` typing composes the frame with no cast. `Part extends object` alone does not forbid a mutable `phase`, so the type parameter cannot carry the guarantee by itself. | replaces C-3's compromise |
| D-10 | **The behavior owns the frame's domain shape.** A feature _may_ contribute an opaque frame part, but the prepare-phase seam it would need is **not specified and not implemented**. | The mechanism is recorded because prohibiting it was wrong; building it for no consumer would be the speculative generality the brief forbids. | new |
| D-11 | Attempt identity: object identity for resolution/readiness/settlement (kernel-private), a monotonic `number` for the coalesced spatial attempt (behavior-private). **D-33's extension is withdrawn with it (D-41, Revision 2): there is no readiness acknowledgement, so attempt identity is kernel-private again without qualification — which is what this row said before Phase 14.** ~~**Extended by D-33 (Checkpoint C, C4-05): the _readiness acknowledgement_ identity is not kernel-private.**~~ It is the `ReorderRequest` the behavior published, held by the consumer, and compared by the behavior in `controller.ready(request)`. The kernel deliberately does not know what a request is — `KernelHost.presentationCommitted()` carries none — so cross-operation safety on that path is a **behavior** obligation, not a kernel one (I-35). The _hold, deadline and settle latch_ stay kernel-private, which is what this row still decides. |  | keeps C-8 |
| D-12 | ~~A feature is `(context) => Contribution`.~~ — **superseded by D-45 (Revision 2).** A fragment is a plain partial config object; the merge is over config slots and the installers it names are invoked after it. What this row still decides survives verbatim: the result is read once at assembly and **dropped**, and it is validatable by key. What changes is that the object is authored by the consumer's own literal rather than returned from a branded factory. | H-4/H-5. Declarative, validatable by key. | replaces C-5; superseded by D-45 |
| D-13 | Features declare **their own structural view types**. No feature imports `Pick<SortableRuntime, …>`. | Inverts the dependency: the consumer states its requirement. | replaces C-3's projections |
| D-14 | The eight-phase vocabulary and the five resource lifetimes are kept verbatim. | Nothing in this model touched them. | keeps C-15 |
| D-15 | The physical frame is composed **kernel-first from parts**: the kernel writes its own literal, then folds the behavior's part. Two sources, no fold over feature parts (D-10). The behavior authors `createFramePart()` / `resetFramePart()` only. | The behavior can no longer mis-initialise kernel fields, because it cannot name them. Construction-time hidden-class transitions are paid twice per controller and are not a measured cost. | new |
| D-16 | ~~The landing target is **provisional while readiness is pending**~~ — **narrowed by D-41 (Revision 2) to: measured once, authoritatively, then pinned at the join.** The serial order guarantees the authored DOM is final before the measurement is taken, so there is no provisional interval, no `authoredReady`, and no readiness-specific retarget. Two clauses survive and are the ones that were load-bearing: **the kernel performs the final pin at the join** before releasing presentation, and **whether to re-anchor follows the recovery**. | Evidenced by the React probe. Correctness comes from the final pin, not from every runner being retargetable. Probe C1 then showed the provisional half was worse than unnecessary: `authoredReady` is false at `armSettlement` **by construction**, so the provisional target was stale in all five commit strategies including the two that otherwise hold. | new; narrowed by D-41 |
| D-17 | **Pointer capture is kernel-owned**, acquired on `root` at activation and released with the motion lifetime. | The kernel already owns pointer identity, ingress, the motion lifetime, release ordering and teardown. This makes `activation.prepare` externally inert for the reference behavior. | new |
| D-18 | The resolution choice is a **staged `ResolutionCommand`**, not a `ResolutionGate` with `open()`/`skip()`. | Makes "exactly one choice, exactly once" structural. A capability with two methods and no state machine left zero calls, duplicate calls and `open` -then- `skip` all undefined. | new |
| D-19 | **Geometry is a paired contribution** — `resolve` / `invalidate` / `retire` — flattened into direct slots at assembly. | The behavior owns the events that make geometry stale; the feature owns the cache. Contributing only `resolve` forced behavior code to reach into a private rect index, which could not compile. | new |
| D-20 | Feature seams take **frame state and runtime state as separate arguments**. The runtime argument is one `PresentationView` per operation. | A single view carrying a `current` frame property was not constructible without leaking the kernel's swappable references. Separating them also removes a duplicated `pointerY`. | narrows D-13 |
| D-21 | `LifetimeScope = Readonly<Pick<Lifetime, 'signal' \| 'use' \| 'useWhile'>>` is what activation hands the behavior. | A type-level projection costs nothing — the same physical object is passed — and it converts I-11 from "the behavior should not close motion" into "it cannot". | new |
| D-22 | The v1 authoring surface is **unsupported-but-possible**, not prevented. Seam, spec and host types are unexported; `SortableFeature` is exported because authoring has to typecheck at all. | Named because the brief asks for the boundary. TypeScript accepts a structurally matching literal regardless of whether a type name is exported, so "only built-ins may author" was never enforceable — and it is not a correctness boundary, since nothing in the kernel depends on provenance. | new |
| D-23 | The seam core returns a **`SeamOutcome`** (discarded / invalidated / prepare-failed / committed / effect-failed), and **a classified failure stops incompatible continuation** — no retirement, no consumer invocation, no gate arming, ~~no terminal callback~~ — **the last clause is retracted by D-66 (Revision 2.1)**: a terminal is **disposition**, not continuation, and a started operation publishes exactly one whatever happened. Everything else on this list stands. | Catching a throw is only half a failure model. The checkpoint is queued, so the window between the throw and the checkpoint is exactly where the old boolean let success work run. | new |
| D-24 | `SettlementInput` is **discriminated and exhaustive over all five cases** — fulfilled / rejected / skipped / canceled / failed — with a stated mapping to outcome, recovery, domain result and callback. | Five _open numeric_ statuses with no mapping was the defect; the number of cases was not. Removing canceled/failed made the kernel unable to produce behavior-owned terminal state at all (D-28). | new |
| D-25 | The collection action **never discards**: it stages `cancelReason` and `effect` publishes first, then cancels. | An invalid collection ends the current drag; it must not throw away the consumer's update, which is what `host.cancel()` + `null` did. | new |
| D-26 | **Post-callback revalidation** at every reentrancy-capable boundary, with stale-return disposal for anything the callback _returns_. | Reserve-before-call protects resources that already exist. A `LandingStart` that destroys the controller and then returns a live handle leaked it; an `admit` resolver that destroys let the listener mint an operation on a terminal controller. | new |
| D-27 | One canonical `movePlaceholder()`, anchored on `insertion.after` with an append fallback, **inert when the placeholder is already in position**, and **refusing an anchor outside the placeholder's own container**; `homeInsertion` carries real identity neighbours. | `before?.after(…)` is a silent no-op for a start gap. `Node.before()`/`append()` on an already-correct position is a remove-and-reinsert that resets CSS transitions and forces layout, and release invokes the helper unconditionally. `before()`/`after()` are relative to the _anchor_, so an item the consumer reparented mid-drag does not fail the write — it moves the placeholder into the other container, taking the drag's layout footprint out of the list. Every caller reaches the writer with an insertion built from a snapshot that may be older than the DOM (spatial move, release write, home recovery), so the refusal belongs in the writer and classifies at each caller's own stage — `PLACEHOLDER_MOVE`, `RELEASE`, `LANDING_TARGET`. Destination recovery does not use the writer and already tests sibling identity before re-anchoring. | new |
| D-28 | **Every classification entrypoint latches.** A kernel-private `seamFailureRequested` flag makes `host.fail` indistinguishable from a throw at the driver boundary; `armSettlement` returns `ARM_ARMED`/`ARM_STALE`/`ARM_FAILED`; landing completion is once-only. | D-23 covered throws only. The checkpoint is _queued_, so the window before it applies is precisely what has to be closed — and it was still open for explicit `host.fail`, for landing-create failure, and for a synchronous runner `fail()`. | extends D-23 |
| D-29 | **Teardown is total.** Each frame reset is individually wrapped and ingress abort runs from a `finally`. **Rescoped by D-36 (Revision 2):** totality is a property of the teardown _sequence_, wherever it runs, not of the stack that called `destroy()`. When teardown defers to the transaction boundary the same seven steps run with the same wrapping; what no longer holds is that they have run by the time `destroy()` returns. | `resetFramePart` is behavior code the API permits to throw; an unwrapped throw made `destroy()` non-terminal against I-6. | new |
| D-30 | ~~The **feature value is opaque** — an unexported `unique symbol` brand.~~ — **retracted by D-45 (Revision 2).** The second clause stands: `FailureStage` and `DOMRealm` are public because public types structurally depend on them. The brand does not. D-45 finds the coherent third state this row said did not exist — **a public stable schema whose slot values are opaque** — so opacity moves from the record to the installer inside it, and third-party authoring of a _config_ becomes ordinary object literal syntax while authoring an _installer_ still requires the kernel entrypoint (D-47). | "Public stable type whose full structure is internal and unstable" is not a coherent third state. Branding closes the world for real; exporting the genuinely-needed types stops the leak the other way. | replaces D-22; retracted by D-45 |
| D-31 | The public proposal and results are **narrowed unions with string discriminants**, carrying version, identity neighbours, rejection reason and cancellation stage. | Probe 1's preserved product contract. Discrimination must not require an internal outcome constant. | new |
| D-32 | Discrete input is a **second admission member**, `command: { types, admit(event, draft) }`, not an intent protocol. The kernel binds the declared types on `root` inside its own ingress abort; the member answers feasibility synchronously in the listener, writes its state into the open draft and returns the element to lift or `null`. A non-null return mints a **pointerless** operation — `pointerId === -1`, no pointer listeners, no capture — which the kernel activates and releases in one slot. `PENDING` is redefined as _activation not yet committed_. | The 13a gap was never "a behavior needs to request a transition"; it was "a behavior needs to be asked a question before anything is queued". A second admission is the whole requirement, and it adds **no** `KernelHost` member, no phase and no frame field. Carrying a staged `Prepared` from the listener — 13a's candidate — would have broken §The staged value never outlives its transaction and forced a second staged type parameter; the draft already carries it. | new |
| D-33 | **Retracted by D-41 (Revision 2). The whole protocol is deleted, not amended** — `ReorderResolution.accept({ presentation: true })`, `controller.ready(request)`, `KernelHost.presentationCommitted()`, `rt.pendingRequest`, the acknowledgement deadline and the four-row invalid-acknowledgement matrix all go. The reasoning below was sound and its premise is gone: D-33 solved _how a consumer acknowledges a render that happens after the resolution returns_, and the serial commit removes that window by having the resolution not return until the render is done. Kept in full because it is the best record of why per-operation identity on a controller method is hard, which D-47's kernel surface will meet again. ~~The authored presentation is **declared in the resolution and acknowledged through the controller**~~: `ReorderResolution.accept({ presentation: true })`, then `controller.ready(request)`, compared by **object identity** against the request the behavior published from `release.effect`. `PreparedSettlement` carries a boolean, `holdForReadiness()` takes nothing, and `KernelHost` gains `presentationCommitted()`. An acknowledgement arriving before the settlement exists is latched on the resolution attempt, and discarded at **seal** — with a report — if the gate plan turns out to hold no readiness. Every acknowledgement that contradicts its own resolution, early or late, is _reported and dropped_, never silently ignored. The declaration is **opt-in**, and omitting it is tier-C discipline rather than a caught error. | Probe 13b's candidate C-3, whose only objection was that a controller method has no per-operation identity — which the **request** already supplies: it is public, per-operation, and in the consumer's hand before the render it acknowledges, because it is what asked for the render. That ordering is why C-2's kernel-minted token failed: a capability created at arm time is younger than a synchronous commit. No settlement primitive crosses the public boundary, nothing is allocated on the path, and `effect` still returns `void`, so the render/landing overlap survives structurally. | replaces the `presentationReady` promise |
| D-34 | `BehaviorSpec<Part, Activation extends {} = true>` — the activation seam's staged type is the **behavior's** choice. The sortable behavior declares `HTMLElement`; a behavior that stages nothing returns `true`. | `Transition` already defaults `Prepared` to `true`; it was `BehaviorSpec` pinning it to `HTMLElement` that made a second behavior return an element it does not own, with `effect` obliged to ignore it. The kernel still treats the staged value as `{}` and drops it, so this is a one-parameter change with a wide re-verification surface and no runtime cost. | new |
| D-35 | `LandingContext.from` is **the delta the lift session last rendered**, read from the session the kernel owns — not `pointerX - originX`. **From acquisition until `from` is sampled**, conforming behavior rendering goes through `write(x, y)`; after that the landing runner is the deliberate writer until `destroy()`. `compose(x, y)` composes and records nothing. | The pointer form is true for exactly one behavior. Any behavior that constrains, clamps, snaps or externally drives its visual — and every pointerless operation under D-32 — starts its landing from a position the visual is not at. Recording inside a kernel-owned method beats the `renderedDelta(current)` seam it replaces: two scalar writes and no seam, writes that do not come from `moved` are still tracked, and no behavior has to implement anything. **Narrowed at Checkpoint C, C4-02**: the interval ends at the sample, not at the join, and "renders only through `write`" is tier-C discipline (I-34). | new |

### Revision 2 — the API redesign (D-36…D-60)

D-61…D-67 follow in §Revision 2.1 — five drift corrections and two owner decisions closing what the reconciliation surfaced.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-36 | **Logical closure and physical teardown are separate events.** `destroy()` closes the controller logically and immediately — every guard fails from that statement onward — and if synchronous library execution is in progress, **physical teardown runs at the boundary of the outermost library transaction**, not on the closing stack. Outside a reentrant transaction the two still coincide. `destroy()` returns `Promise<void>`, settling once, after physical teardown. **`panic()` follows the same model**, and the order becomes _close → report → teardown_. | Probe A: `panic` is reached from `drain`'s `catch` **after** the loop has exited, so the deferral window is one stack frame wide and its only statement is `report`, which touches no library state. Observed as teardown order flipping `['retire','report']` → `['report','retire']`. The bracket is what lets 27 of 33 statement-level liveness readings retire (D-37): a guard exists to stop work that would outlive the close, and work that is undone at the boundary has no consequence to stop. | narrows D-29's totality to the deferred boundary; reverses 01 §Teardown's "physical release completes before it returns" |
| D-37 | **The liveness domain is finite and API-shaped.** After logical closure the library must not (a) invoke a **declared consumer slot**, (b) **admit** another operation, or (c) **publish** another lifecycle or domain event. Internal work already inside the current transaction may finish, provided it survives the boundary in no observable form. The whole-program ceilings — "reads no further geometry", "no further consumer-reachable read" — are **withdrawn**, and the consumer reach/stretch analysis is **retired as a proof domain**. | The old quantifier ranged over _every overridable member of every consumer-owned node_, which 05 itself admits "cannot be enumerated" — an obligation no implementation can discharge and no review can check. Probe A shows why the replacement is not merely weaker: a bracket makes an act consequence-free by **undoing** it, and for a call the consequence _is_ the call, so exactly the declared-slot invocations survive as irreducible residue. The quantifier goes from undischargeable to a finite list the export table already enumerates. | retracts I-36's ceiling register and the 62-row stretch table; retracts README §Terminal barrier's published ceiling |
| D-38 | **No physical-teardown observation may answer a liveness question.** An aborted resource or presentation signal lags logical closure once teardown defers. Liveness is read from the logical latch or from transaction validity, never from `signal.aborted`, a disposed session, or a detached node. | Probe A: the only three failures the bracket caused _on its own_ were `presentation.signal.aborted` used as a liveness reading. Those sites were chosen deliberately because the signal is **strictly stronger** than the closed latch — it also catches a kernel-internal panic. Under deferral the same property makes it strictly **weaker**, because it now lags the close it is standing in for. A reading that was correct for a stated reason became wrong for that same reason. | new — recorded as I-37 |
| D-39 | **A prepared-but-unadopted placeholder must be rolled back.** The sortable's `activation.rollback` is **required**, not vacuous: it undoes library-authored attributes, styles and state written onto a consumer-owned element during `prepare`. | The residue does not dissolve under deferred teardown, which an earlier reading of this revision claimed. `prepare` completes but is never **adopted** — `preparationValid()` returns false and the seam reports `SEAM_INVALIDATED` — so the disposer registered in `effect` never runs and never becomes responsible. Deferral changes when teardown happens, not whether adoption occurred. This is a **local acquisition property** with an existing mechanism; it is not a reason to reinstate statement-level liveness. | reverses 06 §"`activation.rollback` is unnecessary"; corrects I-17's "vacuous for the sortable behavior" |
| D-40 | **One `canceled` terminal. No `aborted`.** **The `onEnd` union has four arms — `accepted`, `noop`, `rejected`, `canceled`** — and any five-arm `EndResult` carrying `aborted` in an earlier redesign draft is superseded here. **`onEnd` is the callback's name as well as the union's, and this row is where that was written down and then not carried into any other document — corrected by D-62.** The four arms are the existing `ReorderTransactionResult`, unchanged. **What this row did not settle is what the fifth arm was _for_: see §The unresolved arm and Q-14.** **Implementation consequence, implied by this row rather than stated by it:** consuming a late settlement safely requires the resolution attempt's `catch` to outlive the attempt's currency, so the abort guard cannot key off `completed` alone. Probe A verified the behavior; the lifetime change is what delivers it. `canceled` says _the drag operation_ was abandoned; it has never promised that consumer side effects were rolled back, and after release it must not be read as one. A settlement arriving from an abandoned resolver is **consumed safely** — no unhandled rejection, no second terminal, no revival. `onError` remains the orthogonal diagnostic channel. | A second terminal name would encode _provenance_, not a different consumer obligation: in both cases the consumer must not assume its own started work was undone, because the library never had a way to undo it. Probe A verified the abandonment path reaches one terminal and consumes the late rejection with a real `unhandledrejection` listener attached. Splitting the name would oblige every consumer to handle two results that call for the same code. | closes the C-2 proposal to add `aborted`; qualifies I-31 |
| D-41 | **The authored commit is serial, and the readiness gate is deleted.** The order is _release → freeze proposal → `onReorder` → authored commit → consumer resolution → restore library presentation invariants → authoritative landing measurement → landing → terminal_. `controller.ready(request)`, `ResolutionOptions`, `KernelHost.presentationCommitted()`, `rt.pendingRequest`, `authoredReady`, the acknowledgement deadline and the readiness-specific retarget lifecycle are **all removed**. Settlement holds **one** gate, landing. A framework commit barrier is integration code, not a drag protocol. | The serial order leaves the readiness hold **no producer**: a consumer that must render before the landing measurement awaits its own commit inside `onReorder`, which is what a Promise-returning resolver already expresses. D-33 existed to let the resolution return _before_ the render and be acknowledged after; deleting that window deletes the protocol, the identity comparison it needed, and the four-row invalid-acknowledgement matrix underneath it. It also dissolves probe C1's finding that the provisional landing target is stale in **all five** commit cases — with one measurement, taken authoritatively, there is no provisional target to go stale. | retracts D-33 and I-35; narrows D-7 to a single gate and D-16 to _measure once, pin at the join_; resolves F-46 and F-6's residue by deletion |
| D-42 | **Destructive rerenders during an active operation are out of contract, and the precondition is checked.** Supported authored commits move existing item nodes and leave the drag-owned DOM intact — an append loop, keyed or morphdom-style movement. `replaceChildren`, `innerHTML` rebuilds, and replacing the container are unsupported. At the authoritative landing measurement the library verifies **O(1)** that the placeholder is still connected and still a sibling of the item; a failed check **reports** through `onError` and lands from the unrepaired position. Nothing is recovered. | Probe C1: three of five commit strategies produce a landing target of `{-50,-100}` — a detached placeholder measures `0×0` at the viewport origin — and the row visibly travels to `(0,0)` over 12 frames before teleporting back. **All five report `onEnd` once and `onError` zero times** (probe C1 measured `onFinish`; D-62 renamed the channel), so the worst integration bug in the package is also its most silent. The check is two reads and is not renderer detection: it validates a precondition the landing measurement already depends on. Recovery is refused separately — C1 shows `ReorderRequest.before`/`after` survive in 4 of 5 cases, so recovery is _possible_, and buying it would mean relaxing D-27's cross-container refusal for a case now outside the contract. | new — F-49 |
| D-43 | **`box()` and `visual()` are separate, and the footprint needs two measurement windows.** `visual` is the node faithfully lifted; `box` is the geometry source; `box(item) = visual(item)` by default. The removed footprint's **height** is **`boxPre.height − boxPost`** when `box` and `visual` differ, measured before the lift and again after the visual leaves flow, and **`boxPre.height` alone when `box === visual`** — corrected during implementation (F-55), because a lifted element's own offset box does not change across `acquireLift`, so the subtraction yields `0` rather than its height in the default composition. Its **width is `boxPre.width` on every composition** — **corrected again at application review 1 (F-58)**: the subtraction measures a _collapse_, which is a scalar on the flow axis, and a box that surrenders no cross extent made it `width: 0px` on every composed `box`. `item.after(placeholder)` and the copied `slot` attribute both stay. Scope limits are stated positively: visual order must follow DOM order; rule-placed layouts are unsupported; a composed `box !== visual` is supported **with `y()` only**, and in grid `box === visual` is required. | api-1 measured it: with a sibling remaining in the box, `boxPre 62 − boxPost 32 = 30`, and the list collapsed by exactly 30 — while `box` (62) and `visual` (60) are both wrong, in different directions in different cases. Probe C1 reproduced it live and found the shipped `visual()` sizing the placeholder from `visual.offsetHeight`, running the list **30 px too tall for an entire drag**. A running translate corrupts the box's top by 60 px and its height by 0, so both windows must be offset-box reads. The timing is free: `acquireLift` and the activation seam are already 40 lines apart. | corrects 03 §`placeholder()`'s single-window "sized from the visual's offset box"; extends D-27's anchoring rationale |
| D-44 | **Collection delivery is a pull source plus a payload-free invalidation.** `sortable({ items: () => … })` supplies the current committed collection; `controller.invalidate()` says the committed presentation or data may have changed. `updateItems(payload)` is **removed**, and no `itemCount()`/`itemAt()` scanning protocol replaces it. **Array identity is the structural-change signal**: the same identity invalidates geometry only, a new identity triggers snapshot, reconcile and geometry invalidation. The library's shallow copy happens **only** on the structural branch. In-place mutation of the same array is outside the contract. | The thunk was called once at construction and every later change went through `updateItems`, so the package carried two collection channels and re-read neither. One source plus one signal collapses them, and array identity is a free structural test the consumer already produces — React, Vue and Svelte all return a new array when order changes. This puts the O(n) copy on structural change rather than on every warm invalidation, which is what a resize or zoom produces. The name collides with the feature-owned geometry `invalidate()` on purpose: the controller member is the **cause**, the geometry slot the **effect**. | retracts `updateItems()` and ledger L-1's "the thunk is called exactly once, at construction" |
| D-45 | **A fragment is a plain declarative partial config.** `y()` returns `{ axis: installYAxis }`; `layoutAnimation()` returns `{ plugins: [installLayoutAnimation()] }`. The call stays **variadic and the library merges** — `sortable(root, config, y(), landing())` — with merge semantics owned by the **config slot**, not by fragment provenance: scalar and capability slots last-wins, `plugins` appends in fragment order. Installers are invoked **after** the merge completes, so a losing capability is never constructed. Defaults are derived post-merge. The **opaque feature brand is withdrawn**; what stays opaque is the _installer value_, not the record that carries it. | D-30 branded the feature value because "public stable type whose full structure is internal and unstable" is not a coherent third state. A plain config record is the coherent state D-30 could not find: **the schema is public and stable, the installer values are opaque**, and a consumer can therefore spread a preset, override one slot, filter a plugin array, or lift `weirdThing().axis` out of a helper — none of which requires constructing an installer. ~~which still needs the kernel entrypoint (D-47).~~ **That clause is a drift and is corrected by D-61**: constructing an installer needs the **middle** tier, `sortable/feature.js`, not the kernel. Sending an installer author to the kernel entrypoint tells them to replace the sortable in order to extend it. Keeping the merge in the library is what keeps `plugins` appending; a consumer-merged single object would silently last-wins the one slot that must concatenate. | retracts D-30 and the `SortableFeature` brand; supersedes D-12's contribution objects and the C-5 strategy/plugin tag resolution |
| D-46 | **Two gaps closed at Phase R, both recorded in 02 §Input policy: the opt-out attribute is `data-drag-ignore`, and the command table is enumerated and is deliberately narrower than the pointer one** — an `a[href]` or a `button` owns no arrow key, so declining there would remove keyboard reordering from a focused control for nothing. **Ingress must not consume interaction it does not use.** `preventDefault()` at admission is withdrawn as an unconditional act: a press that never crosses the activation threshold must leave native focus, caret, selection and form-control behavior intact. Default admission **declines** on interactive and editable descendants, so the existing decline path — which probe E confirms leaves native behavior completely untouched — carries the policy. Keyboard command admission must ask what the event landed on: text inputs keep caret arrows, `contenteditable` keeps editing navigation, native controls keep their keys, and **`event.isComposing === true` never admits**. Plain-text selection inside a draggable non-interactive region is requested with a modifier rather than inferred. | Probe E: eight of ten cases release-blocking from two root causes, and six of those **never activated a drag at all** — the damage is done by the admission `preventDefault()` alone. The worst case is an `ArrowRight` in a nested text input leaving the caret frozen, `defaultPrevented`, and a complete accepted reorder `{from:2,to:3}` from one keystroke, because the command ingress never asks what the event landed on. This is a policy change, not new machinery: declining is already total, and only `pointerdown` was ever prevented, so click, `href` and ctrl-click already survive. `handle()` resolves seven of the ten but is **not** the accessibility answer — it removes keyboard reordering unless the consumer makes the grip focusable, which is now a stated obligation. | corrects 02 §"moving the call one frame outward produces the same observable result"; adds the interactive-descendant rule 02, 03 and 05 do not currently contain |
| D-59 | **The box element arrives through admission, in the kernel's own vocabulary.** `admit` returns the element to lift, optionally paired with a box element; the kernel stores `box` in **its own per-operation state** beside `originRect` — **held by the kernel, not placed on the transactional frame**, on the same argument that keeps gate state on the settlement attempt: written once at admission, read twice, never transactional. A literal frame field would take the published seven-field slice to eight for no transactional benefit. **The spelling is `HTMLElement \| Readonly<{ visual, box }> \| null`**, discriminated by `'visual' in subject`rather than`instanceof HTMLElement`, because `instanceof`is realm-sensitive and`DOMRealm`exists precisely because an element may come from another document. An optional`box` was rejected: it gives _"the box is the visual"_ two encodings, which this contract refuses everywhere else. It does not read a behavior-authored draft field. | D-52's first form had the behavior stash `box` in the draft and the kernel read it back, which contradicts **H-2** — _the kernel does not know, store, extend or type_ the behavior's part — and D-15, which exists so the kernel cannot name behavior fields. The contradiction is not cosmetic: an exception here is precisely the "kernel learns one sortable-shaped thing" defect Checkpoint C found four times. Widening the admission return keeps D-5's principle intact — _the kernel gets what it needs from admission and nothing else_ — and it now needs two things rather than one, which is a change in the count, not in the rule. Admission runs once per press and is not the hot path, so the pair costs nothing measurable. **This is an SPI seam-signature change — the second and last in Revision 2 — and the freeze rule governs it.** It is discharged by a chain, not by a single probe, and the chain is worth writing out because "it repairs another decision" is not on its own sufficient: **api-1 measured that no single-window rule reproduces the removed footprint in both nested cases** → D-43 needs two windows → the second window requires the box element to be known **before** `acquireLift` → the only two places that can carry it are the behavior's draft and admission's return → the draft form contradicts H-2 and D-15 → admission's return is what is left. The failing executable case is api-1's; D-59 is where it finally lands. | repairs D-52; preserves H-2 and D-15 |
| D-60 | **`onError` is orthogonal to the terminal, and a classified stage may have no recovery.** One operation may produce `onError` **and** `onEnd` (`onFinish` when this row was written — D-62). `FAILURE_LANDING_TARGET` under D-49 is the first stage that is _classified, non-consequential, and has no recovery_ — the settlement is not failed and the domain result stands. | The owner already decided the first half in §4 — _"diagnostics remain orthogonal … do not create a second terminal taxonomy merely to encode diagnostic provenance"_ — but the contract carried an **unstated biconditional**, `onError ⇒ consequential`, that nothing had ever needed to test. D-49 falsifies it, so it has to be written down rather than left as an assumption a reader will re-derive. The second half follows: §Failure classification lists every stage with a recovery, and this one has none, which the `FailureStage` → recovery mapping must be able to express instead of treating as a gap. **Consequence for the test suite, worth stating because probe C1's finding is phrased the other way:** the defect was _`onFinish` once, `onError` zero_, and the fixed behavior is _`onEnd` once, `onError` once_ — so any assertion of mutual exclusivity between the two channels is now false. **Read with D-64**: the assertion the test makes about `onError` is now about a `code`, not a stage, so C1's regression test asserts `onEnd` once and one `DraggableError` whose code is the presentation class. | follows from D-49; makes owner §4 normative |
| D-56 | **A fragment factory that names no installer is deleted.** `callbacks()`, `handle()`, `visual()` and `placeholder()` become plain config keys — `{ onReorder, threshold }`, `{ handle: fn }`, `{ visual: fn }`, `{ box: fn }`, `{ placeholder: fn }` (D-65) — written directly in the config object. Their subpaths go with them. ~~**`placeholder()` carried two things, and both survive as flat keys**: `createPlaceholder` and `placeholderClassName`.~~ **Both are deleted and replaced by one slot, `placeholder` (D-65).** The argument below was mine, not the owner's, and the owner has overruled it; it is kept because it states the cost D-65 accepts. ~~The second is not dead weight — it is the only way to keep the default element and still brand it, and without it that consumer has to supply a whole factory to add one class. It stays **flat rather than nested** because `SortableContribution` is deliberately one flat type with fixed key names, and a single nested slot would be the sole exception to that.~~ The flat-versus-nested argument survives D-65 and now selects a **single callback slot** rather than two keys, which is the shape `handle`, `visual` and `box` already have. The surviving fragment factories are exactly those that install something: **`y()`, `xy()`, `landing()`, `layoutAnimation()`** — and `landing()`'s options narrow to `{ duration, easing }` under D-63. | Under D-45 those four became identity wrappers returning a public config slot, which voids the stated reason their subpaths exist — _"a separate subpath per optional feature is what makes the measurement honest"_. A subpath carrying no runtime machinery measures nothing. Keeping them would leave four exports whose entire behavior is to build an object literal the consumer can write more clearly by hand, which is the ceremony D-45 exists to remove; the argument that retired the feature brand retires these for the same reason one row down. **Validation is not lost**: `callbacks()`'s construction-time `onReorder` requirement and `threshold` check move to the merge, where D-45 already puts the missing-axis check, and they now fire for a config supplied any way at all rather than only through a factory. | follows from D-45; removes four exports and four subpaths |
| D-57 | **Post-merge installation order is schema order; plugins install in array order; retirement is the reverse of installation.** | D-45 erases provenance, so "declaration order" — the order retirement was _defined_ against — no longer names anything, and nothing in the owner's decisions replaces it. Schema order is the only candidate that is deterministic, independent of how the consumer spread their fragments, and stable across a refactor that reorders arguments. The alternative, first-appearance order across the merged fragments, reintroduces exactly the provenance tracking D-45 deleted. | closes the ordering D-45 left undefined |
| D-58 | **Insertion candidates are measured as `box`, not `visual`.** This supersedes Checkpoint D's parity D2, which chose `visual` because no `box` concept existed. | D-43 defines `box` as _the sortable geometry source_ and `visual` as _the node faithfully lifted_, and insertion is a geometry question. Leaving candidates on `visual` while the placeholder occupies the box's removed footprint puts the two sides of the same comparison on different rects — in api-1's case A they differ by **30 px** — which is a hysteresis defect, not a rounding one: the incumbent placeholder is measured one way and its challengers another. Under the default `box === visual` nothing changes, so this costs the common case nothing and makes the uncommon case coherent. D2's _coherence_ argument is what selects `box` here; only its choice of node is superseded. | supersedes parity D2; reconciles D-43 with the axis rule |
| D-55 | **`Behavior<Controller>` and `brandBehavior` are retired from the public surface.** The construction types survive internally. The kernel tier publishes `BehaviorFactory` and the seam vocabulary; it does not publish a branded, packaged behavior value. | D-48 leaves the brand with **no producer**: `sortable()` and `freeDrag()` return controllers, and `draggable()` takes a factory. An exported opaque type that nothing constructs is the same defect D-45 removed one row up — a boundary marker with no boundary to mark. Retiring it also removes the last consumer of `unbrandBehavior` on the install path. If a later behavior genuinely needs to be _packaged without being installed_, that is a new capability with a new justification, not a survival of this one. | follows from D-48; parallels D-45's retraction of the feature brand |
| D-51 | **D-37 (a) admits relinquishing invocations, and they are a closed named list.** A call into a declared consumer slot after logical closure is permitted **only** when its sole purpose is to release a resource the library is holding and cannot release itself. Such a call performs no operation work, publishes nothing, ignores any return value, and is wrapped. **The list is currently one member: `LandingHandle.destroy()`.** Adding to it is a contract change. **D-63 makes the member tier-dependent, and the list survives because the exception is about ownership rather than about consumers.** With the sortable's runner library-owned, `LandingHandle.destroy()` at the ordinary tier is a call into the library's own WAAPI runner and needs no exception at all. At the **kernel** tier a third-party behavior still supplies the runner, and there the call is into code the library does not own — which is what D-37 (a) is actually about. Read "declared consumer slot" throughout D-37 and D-51 as **"a slot filled by code the library does not own"**, which includes a third-party behavior; the narrower reading would silently empty this list the moment the sortable stopped supplying runners, and leave I-20 and F-30 unguarded at the only tier where they can still be violated. Under D-36 a relinquishing call **reached after logical closure** is part of physical teardown and therefore **defers with it**. **Qualified before implementation:** a relinquishing call on the **normal path of a live controller** — the join's `landing.destroy()`, which sits between the authoritative measurement and the pin — is ordinary sequenced work and keeps its position. An earlier unqualified wording would have deferred it past the pin, inverting the one ordering §Landing states as normative. This clause is about _closure_, never about relinquishment as such. | Without this row D-37 is false as written, and falsely in the one place it must not be: the kernel _must_ destroy a landing handle returned by a runner that destroyed the controller, or it leaks a runner nothing owns (I-20, F-30). The old prose floor admitted this as a breach; D-37's finite domain leaves no room for a breach, which is worse — **a finite domain with an unstated exception is not finite**, and the whole value of D-37 is that the list can be checked. So the exception is stated, enumerated, and given a discriminating property: relinquishment _returns_ something to the consumer, it does not _ask_ anything of them. That is why it cannot be used to smuggle operation work past a close. | closes the hole 05 found in D-37; keeps I-20 and F-30 intact |
| D-52 | **The two measurement windows have distinct owners, and neither disturbs `originRect`.** The behavior stashes the `box` element in the admission draft; the **kernel** reads `boxPre` beside its existing `originRect` capture, before `acquireLift`; the **behavior** reads `box.offsetHeight` — **one extent, not a pair** (F-58), because the cross axis never subtracts — at the top of `activation.prepare` — **and skips that read entirely when `box === visual`**, which F-55 corrects: the lifted element's own offset box is unchanged across `acquireLift`, so window 2 is only meaningful when the box is a distinct element that stays in flow. The placeholder-sizing writes stay in `prepare` and are covered by D-39's rollback ledger. `originRect` is **not** derived from either window. | D-39 and D-43 legislate the same code and neither said which seam owns which write, so they could not both be implemented. The ordering makes it free: `acquireLift` precedes the activation seam, so `boxPost` is already available where `prepare` measures today, and the draft already carries behavior state, so stashing `box` needs no new SPI member (D-5). **`originRect` must stay the visual's grab rect**: it is the basis of the origin-relative landing space, and deriving it from a `box` chosen for geometry reasons would make the landing coordinate space a function of a `box()` the consumer picked for layout — which contradicts §One coordinate space. The three rects collapse to one read under the default `box === visual`, which is the common case. | reconciles D-39 with D-43; preserves the frozen landing space |
| D-53 | **`KernelHost` gains a logical-liveness reader.** One readonly member answering _is this controller logically closed_ — the latch itself, not a proxy for it. This is the sanctioned reading D-38 requires, and the only one. | D-38 forbids physical-teardown observations, and after that prohibition **there is no reading left**: the latch is kernel-private and `KernelHost` exposed none, so a behavior could only mirror it by hand. Probe A supplies the failing executable case the freeze rule asks for — the three `presentation.signal.aborted` sites exist _precisely because_ no logical reading was available, and they are the only failures the bracket caused on its own. Requiring every behavior to maintain a private mirror is worse now than before D-47, because the kernel is published and a third-party behavior author would have to know to do it. **This is the only SPI _addition_ in Revision 2 — but not the only SPI change.** D-59 widens `admit`'s return, which is a seam signature. Two crossings, discharged differently: this one by probe A directly, D-59 by api-1 through a chain. An earlier wording of this row called it the sole SPI change, and that was the third of three false single-crossing claims in this revision. | forced by D-38; one of Revision 2's two SPI changes |
| D-54 | **On the pointer path, `preventDefault()` moves to activation, and the drag suppresses its trailing `click`.** The default is not prevented at admission (D-46). It is prevented when the activation threshold is crossed, and at that moment the library also clears any selection the pre-threshold press began. After an **activated** drag the library suppresses exactly one subsequent `click`, in the capture phase. **This is a pointer-path rule and does not reach `command.admit`.** A `keydown` default cannot be prevented once its listener has returned, and a command has no threshold to relocate to — D-32 redefined `PENDING` precisely because the threshold is a pointer-path property. Command admission keeps preventing inside the listener, which is sound because under D-46 command admission **is** the intent test: it has already asked what the event landed on before it answers. **The `click` suppressor's lifetime:** armed at activation, owned by the controller's ingress lifetime rather than the operation's, because the `click` arrives after the operation has ended; disarmed by the first `click`, by the next `pointerdown`, or by teardown, whichever comes first. **A drag cancelled after activation still suppresses** — the browser synthesizes the `click` regardless, and a cancellation is a library or consumer verdict, not evidence the user meant to click. Scroll suppression is **not** `preventDefault()`'s job — it is `touch-action`, which the consumer sets in CSS. | D-46 withdraws the admission call and names no replacement, which leaves the policy incomplete in a consumer-visible way: without relocation nothing prevents the default at all, and without the trailing-`click` suppressor a drag that ends on a link navigates. Both are consequences of moving the call later, not pre-existing defects, so the policy has to carry them. `touch-action` is named explicitly because `preventDefault()` on `pointerdown` was never a reliable scroll suppressor and treating it as one is how the touch story silently breaks. **Evidence limit, stated because the rule is unconditional: probe E is Chromium and mouse only.** Touch adds long-press context menus and tap highlighting that admission's `preventDefault()` also consumed, and nothing has measured them — recorded as an owed measurement, not as a settled case. | completes D-46; supersedes 02's admission-time `preventDefault` |
| D-49 | **A landing that cannot be measured is skipped, not faked.** With one authoritative measurement (D-41), a failed measurement — a throw, or D-42's precondition check finding the placeholder detached or no longer the item's sibling — **reports through `onError`, skips the landing animation, and joins immediately**. It does not fail the settlement, and it does not animate toward a target it does not trust. The drop still terminates normally with its domain result, because the DOM commit has already happened and the reorder is real. | D-41 collapses two measurement sites into one, and that silently converts a tolerated fault into a fatal one: the old contract survived a failed `anchorTarget` per drop, because F-17 and I-29 make it best-effort and the join's pin decides correctness. With one site, failing the settlement (`ARM_FAILED`) would tolerate none. Skipping restores F-17's tier — the failure is **quality only**, exactly as F-16 classifies a visually abrupt correction — while keeping I-31's single terminal. It also **unifies with D-42**: a detached placeholder is precisely the case where an animation must not run, and this replaces D-42's weaker "lands from the unrepaired position" with the behavior probe C1 shows is wanted, since the unrepaired position is the viewport origin and flying to it over 12 frames is the visible defect. A jump cut is honest; a confident animation to `(0,0)` is not. | narrows D-41; supersedes D-42's landing clause; keeps F-16/F-17/I-29's tiering |
| D-50 | **Explicit consumer scoping wins over the default decline.** D-46 declines interactive and editable descendants by default; where the consumer has scoped dragging with `handle()`, the resolved subject governs and the default decline does not re-veto it. The test walks the path between the event target and the resolved subject. | The owner's direction is _"unless the consumer explicitly scopes dragging to that interaction"_, which states a precedence without stating its mechanism. Without this row the two rules are simply in conflict for a grip placed inside an editable region — a real case, since a drag handle inside a rich-text block is ordinary. The consequence is that `handle()` stops being the _only_ descendant-scoping mechanism, which document 03 currently asserts; it becomes the **override** for a policy that now exists by default. | resolves D-46 against `handle()`; corrects 03's sole-mechanism claim |
