| D-48 | **The behavior owns the root, and `draggable()` moves to the kernel tier.** `sortable(root, config, y(), landing())` returns a `SortableController` **directly** and calls `draggable()` internally; `freeDrag(root, …)` takes the same shape. `draggable()`, `BehaviorFactory` and the behavior-construction types are published at `@ydinjs/drag/kernel`, where D-47's `draggable(root, (host) => ({ spec, controller }))` is literally the supported form. **Corrected before implementation: `drag.js` does _not_ become the kernel entrypoint.** An earlier wording of this row said it did, and that was wrong in the one way D-47 exists to prevent — `drag.js` carries `FailureStage`, `Point`, `DOMRealm` and the `FAILURE_*` constants, which `sortable.js`'s `onError` structurally depends on, so an ordinary consumer would have had to import from the **kernel** tier to `switch` on an error stage its own handler was handed. §11 names a **subpath**, `@ydinjs/drag/kernel`, and the entry topology follows it literally: **`drag.js` stays the shared vocabulary** — errors, `Point`, `DOMRealm`, and L-11's three future sentinels — and a **new `kernel.js` carries `draggable()` and the authoring types**. **The premise of that correction is void and the conclusion survives (D-64):** the ordinary consumer no longer receives a `FailureStage` at all, so `drag.js` is not kept for the reason written here. It is kept because `DraggableError` is a **class** — a runtime value both tiers hand out and neither owns. **The entry count is now eight, not seven** (D-61 adds `sortable/feature.js`), and the tiers are three rather than two. Shared vocabulary belongs to neither tier, so it gets its own home instead of lodging in whichever tier also happens to need it. | Two contradictions in Revision 2 turn out to be one. D-47's snippet passes a bare factory, which **C4-03 normatively rejected** for publishing `BehaviorFactory` and both private generic parameters at the public boundary — and D-45 §8 writes `sortable(root, …)` while the contract composes `draggable(list, sortable(items, …))`, naming the element twice. Both dissolve if §11's disclosure ladder is read literally: **the public boundary moves.** C4-03's objection was about leaking low-level vocabulary into the ordinary surface, and at the kernel tier that vocabulary _is_ the surface — publishing it there is D-47's whole point, not a leak. The alternative, an exported `defineBehavior` brander, preserves the handshake equally well but leaves both §8 and §11 wrong as written and makes the consumer write two calls where the shipped package wrote one. **Parity improves as a side effect**: `sortable(container, options)` becomes `sortable(container, config, …fragments)` rather than a two-call composition. | resolves D-45 §8 against D-47; moves C4-03's boundary rather than overruling it |
| D-47 | **Custom behavior authoring stays public and factory-shaped.** The progressive disclosure is `sortable()` / `freeDrag()` → sortable config **and feature** utilities → `@ydinjs/drag/kernel`. **The middle rung is a published surface, not a description of the built-in fragments** — D-61; this row's original wording read as the latter and is what let the rung be deleted. The low-level boundary keeps the two-phase handshake `draggable(root, (host) => ({ spec, controller }))`, because the host must exist before a behavior can build host-backed runtime state, while ingress must not arm until the complete spec and controller have been returned. A preconstructed host-free behavior object cannot replace it. | Publication was never the open question — the minimum public **vocabulary** is. D-1 already made the handshake structural, and inverting it would make "no input before install returns" a rule again instead of an unexpressible state. This row exists so that minimizing the exported vocabulary is not mistaken for deferring the surface. | confirms D-1 and D-2; withdraws the proposal to leave the kernel unpublished |

### Revision 2.1 — the reconciliation (D-61…D-65)

Five owner-decision drifts, corrected. Each row names the owner text it restores, because that — not a new argument — is its warrant.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-61 | **The middle tier is a published, supported surface.** The ladder has three rungs and the second one is real: `sortable()`/`freeDrag()` → **sortable feature and config utilities** → `@ydinjs/drag/kernel`. A new subpath, **`sortable/feature.js`**, publishes the installer vocabulary — `SortableInstaller`, `FeatureContext`, `SortableContribution` and the view types they name — so a third party can author a custom axis, landing or plugin **without writing a behavior**. 03's identity clause 3 (_"the module that declares the installer slot types is not a declared package subpath"_) is **retracted**: it existed to make installer values unconstructible from outside, and constructing them from outside is precisely what this rung is for. | The owner states the ladder in final §11 and in review 3 §1 as `sortable extension author → sortable/feature-utils`, and Revision 2 deleted the rung twice over — D-45's closing sentence sends an installer author to `@ydinjs/drag/kernel`, and 03 §Fragments are public, installers are opaque leaves it as _"if installer authoring is ever supported"_. Both are drifts, and the first is the consequential one: **the kernel tier is where you go to replace the sortable, not to extend it.** A consumer who wants a custom insertion axis would have had to reimplement the collection model, the placeholder, the landing protocol and the release proposal to get one — an overshoot of the entire behavior for one slot. The middle tier exists so the answer to _"my list needs a different axis rule"_ is one installer, not a second sortable. **The cost is the one D-45 already accepted for `SortableConfig`, extended:** `SortableContribution` and `FeatureContext` become a semver surface, and by the closure rule this table runs on, publishing them publishes what they structurally name. Minimizing the middle tier therefore means **narrowing `SortableContribution` itself** — the same tension D-48 records for `BehaviorSpec`, now present at two rungs instead of one. | restores owner final §11 and review 3 §1; retracts 03's identity clause 3 and D-45's "drops to `@ydinjs/drag/kernel`" |
| D-62 | **One public terminal callback, `onEnd(result)`.** `onFinish` and `onCancel` are deleted; the four arms `accepted`, `noop`, `rejected`, `canceled` are the existing `ReorderTransactionResult` union, which is already exactly those four. `SortableFinishResult` and `SortableCancelResult` are deleted with the callbacks — they exist only to partition that union for two callback signatures, and there is one signature now. `onError` stays orthogonal (D-60). | D-40 already decided this — _"the `onEnd` union has four arms"_ — and named no other channel, but every other document kept the pair, so the contract set said `onEnd` in one row and `onFinish`/`onCancel` in eighty. The rename is not cosmetic: **it deletes a defect class rather than renaming one.** F-37 is _"`finalized` used a binary accepted-vs-everything predicate, sending the no-op result to `onCancel`"_ — a routing bug that is only possible when a four-arm result has to be split across two callbacks by a predicate. One callback taking the whole union has no predicate and no routing to get wrong, and D-24's exhaustive discriminant becomes the consumer's switch instead of the library's. It also makes the owner's lifecycle pairing statable in the terms it is written in: _"if `onStart` fires and the controller remains alive, the operation eventually produces one terminal `onEnd`."` | completes D-40; dissolves F-37's mechanism; supersedes 02 §Callbacks' two-callback routing |
| D-63 | **The public custom landing runner is removed from the sortable surface.** `LandingOptions` is `{ duration?, easing? }`; `landing({ run })` and the public `LandingStart`/`LandingHandle`/`LandingContext` exports go. The library owns the landing animation through WAAPI. **Nothing is removed from the kernel**: the seam is unchanged, the kernel still never names a runner type, and a kernel-tier behavior author still supplies one — review 3 §10 says so in as many words. | Standing owner decision from review 1 §5, reaffirmed in review 2 and review 3 §10, and Revision 2 carried none of it: D-56 lists `landing()` among the surviving factories without touching its options, and 03 §`landing()` still documents `run` as _"a first-class citizen"_. The removal is a **tier move, not a capability loss**, which is why it costs the contract almost nothing: the protocol that made a custom runner safe — reserve the hold, seal, then arm, latch completion once — is what makes the _library's own_ WAAPI runner safe, and it stays. What actually leaves the ordinary tier is a **public lifecycle protocol** the owner judged not worth its surface: four types, a handle contract, a completion latch and a relinquishment obligation, all so a consumer could write a spring. | restores review 1 §5, review 2 and review 3 §10; narrows D-56's factory list; re-scopes the preserved-from-probe-1 runner row to the kernel tier |
| D-64 | ~~**Consumer error codes are coarse strings; failure stages stay internal.**~~ **Vocabulary half reversed by D-132, 2026-08-26; tier half stands.** `onError` receives a `DraggableError extends Error` carrying a `code: DraggableErrorCode` — currently `'consumer' \| 'interaction' \| 'presentation' \| 'platform'`, names not frozen — and the native `cause`. The **13 `FAILURE_*` constants and `FailureStage` leave the ordinary tier**; they survive internally and are published at the kernel tier, where a behavior author classifies with them. A **total** stage → code mapping is required, and it is library-owned. **The count is thirteen, and this row said fourteen until Revision 2 closure** (A-8): `FAILURE_PRESENTATION_READY = 13` was deleted with the readiness protocol by D-41, and its number was deliberately not reused, so the stage list is thirteen constants whose highest value is 14. The implementation, `tests/exports.node.test.ts` and `tests/kernel/errors.node.test.ts` all say thirteen; this row was the only document that did not. `CancelStage` (`AT_PROPOSAL`/`AT_CONSUMER`) is **not** affected — it is a cancellation stage on a domain result, not a failure stage, and review 3 §12 targets `FAILURE_*` only. **What survives of this row and what does not** (D-132): the coarse `code`, the stages' departure from the ordinary tier and the total mapping are all reversed. What stands is the **tier** argument — `DraggableError` belongs on the shared root, an ordinary consumer never imports `kernel.js`, and one name has one declaration — and the count correction, which D-130 then took to twelve. The stages return to the ordinary tier by a _second publication point_ rather than by moving back, so this row's inversion guard holds under both. | Review 3 §12, unrecorded by Revision 2: _"consumer error codes describe actionable fault classes, not internal pipeline seams."_ The 13 stages are granular on the pipeline axis and silent on the only axis a consumer acts on — _is this my bug or the library's_ — which is what the four codes encode. **This voids D-48's stated rationale and leaves its conclusion standing**, and the distinction has to be explicit or the next reader will re-derive the entry topology from an argument that no longer holds: D-48 kept `drag.js` as a third root because _"`sortable.js`'s `onError` hands the consumer a `FailureStage`"_, and it no longer does. `drag.js` survives on a **different shared symbol** — `DraggableError` is a class, so it is a runtime value both tiers need, and a kernel-tier author must not import it from `sortable.js`. Three roots, same shape, second derivation. That the conclusion survived two independent arguments is worth exactly as much caution as it is comfort: it has now been reached twice and measured never. | restores review 3 §12; voids D-48's rationale while preserving its conclusion; supersedes 03 §The public/internal boundary's "public and stable at the ordinary tier: `FailureStage` … and the `FAILURE_*` constants" |
| D-65 | **`placeholder` is the consumer callback slot.** `placeholder(context): HTMLElement` returns a fresh detached element; once adopted the library owns it for the operation. `createPlaceholder` and `placeholderClassName` are both deleted. | Review 3 §2's config schema and §4 both spell it `placeholder`, and §4 states the negative case explicitly — the slot is the callback, not a record with a `create` member. D-56 split it into two flat keys and argued at length for the second; **the owner has overruled that, and the loss is real rather than notional**: a consumer who wants the default element with one class of their own must now supply a factory that builds the element. That is three lines instead of one, for the case D-56 predicted would be common. It is accepted because the alternative is two slots for one concern, and because a single callback is the shape every other consumer-supplied node in this config already has — `handle`, `visual`, `box` are all `(item) => Element`. | restores review 3 §2 and §4; supersedes D-56's `createPlaceholder` + `placeholderClassName` split |
| D-66 | **`onEnd` is exactly-once domain disposition; `onError` is orthogonal.** Every started operation on a still-live controller publishes **one** `onEnd`, on the failure path as well as the success path. Its argument is **the domain result the frame already holds, and `canceled` when it holds none** — existing result wins, otherwise `canceled`, with the classifying error as the cancellation `reason`. 02 §The join's _"the terminal callback is skipped after a consequential failure"_ and the rule that _"a failed operation reports through `onError` **only**"_ are both **retracted**. **D-23's list loses exactly one item** — _"no terminal callback"_ — and keeps the rest. | Q-14's three-way conflict, resolved by the owner. The distinction that makes it coherent is between **continuation** and **disposition**: D-23 stops a classified failure from doing more _operation work_ — no retirement, no gate arming, no seam success path — and a terminal callback is not operation work, it is the statement of how the operation ended. Skipping it made the library's most serious failures its quietest, which is the defect probe C1 found by a different route: _`onFinish` once, `onError` zero_ was invisible, and _no terminal at all_ is invisible in the same way. **The old reason inverts into the new rule.** The skip existed because the frame still said `OUTCOME_ACCEPTED` and publishing would announce a successful drop; but when the authored commit has already run, the drop **is** accepted — the reorder is real and permanent — and the consumer must be told so. `onEnd` answers _what happened to the data_, `onError` answers _what went wrong_, and neither substitutes for the other. **No SPI change, and the carrier already exists** — but it is **not** `reportFailure`, which an earlier wording of this row named and which is admission-only (_"a failure with **no operation to settle**: `admit` threw, so identity was never minted"_). The in-operation carrier is **`SettlementInput` with `SETTLED_FAILED`**, which already carries `{ stage, error }`, and the failure checkpoint already routes **every** classified failure of a live operation through the settlement seam to deliver it. **`settlement.prepare` writes the fallback into `draft.domain` and returns the existing `PreparedSettlement` sentinel**; `effect` receives a `Readonly` frame and cannot write frame state at all. `finalized` then publishes `current.domain` and nothing else. **Nothing is added to the frame part or to `PreparedSettlement`, so [04](04-frame-slicing.md)'s frame model is unchanged by this row** — the fallback occupies a field that has been `ReorderTransactionResult \| null` since D-24. The two facts the kernel does not supply are carried by a behavior-private progress marker — §D-66 §The progress marker, below. | resolves Q-14; retracts 02 §The join's skip and the `onError`-only rule; narrows D-23 by one clause; makes I-31 unconditional except for `destroy()` |
| D-67 | **Landing duration is contextual, and the zero-argument thunk is deleted.** `landing({ duration })` accepts a number or **`duration({ distance, from, to }): number`**, invoked once per landing at settlement and validated against the same domain as the fixed form. `duration: () => number` is removed **from the documented surface**; it is not, and cannot be, a compile error — see F-52. `from` and `to` are the landing's origin-relative deltas, `distance` the straight-line magnitude between them. | Review 3 §10 rejected the zero-argument thunk on its own terms — _"it cannot even observe the distance that motivated dynamic timing"_ — and deferred the contextual form to a proven need. **D-63 supplied the need by removing the alternative.** With `landing({ run })` gone the thunk was the sole surviving carrier of parity L-6, so the three options were: keep a thunk the owner had already judged unjustified, delete it and lose a shipped capability outright, or ship the contextual form. The third is the only one that both discharges §10 and keeps L-6, and it costs nothing the thunk did not already cost: the invocation site, the validation and the reduced-motion ordering are unchanged — **only the argument list grows**, from zero to one object the landing already has in hand at that moment. `from` and `to` are already computed for `LandingContext`; `distance` is one `Math.hypot`. | closes the L-6 conflict D-63 opened; discharges review 3 §10's second clause; supersedes the `() => number` form in D-63's surviving options |

#### D-66 §The progress marker

Out of the table because it is a mechanism rather than a decision, and because a table row is the wrong place for one.

**Two facts the kernel does not supply are carried by one behavior-private monotone marker** — `MINTED → STARTED → RESOLVING`, per operation, cleared in `retire()`. It is advanced at two sites the behavior already writes:

| State | Advanced | The fallback publishes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MINTED` | the operation exists | **nothing** — 02 §No start, no terminal |
| `STARTED` | **immediately before invoking `onStart`** | `canceled` at `AT_PROPOSAL` |
| `RESOLVING` | as the first statement of the `ResolutionCommand.invoke` closure | `canceled` at `AT_CONSUMER` |

`RESOLVING` is truthful by construction: the kernel runs that closure _only_ after `release.effect` returns normally, and `invoke: null` means no round-trip at all. Deriving it from `proposal !== null` is **false** — the proposal commits one seam earlier, in `release.prepare`.

`STARTED` advances **before** the call, not after, so that a throw **from `onStart` itself** still publishes a terminal. The consumer has been told the drag began; it is owed an end.

**Pinned, and deliberately not broadened: a failure classified before that point publishes `onError` and no `onEnd`.** The owner's guarantee is an implication over _started_ operations, and D-66 does not make it a biconditional (02 §No start, no terminal).

#### D-62 §The unresolved arm — **resolved by D-66**

> **Resolved, 2026-08-13, by the owner: `onEnd` is exactly-once domain disposition, `onError` orthogonal, existing result wins and `canceled` otherwise.** The section below is kept because it is the record of what the conflict was and why the three candidate answers were not equivalent — and because the answer taken is the third one, which **inverts** the stated reason 02 §Landing (D-16) gave, rather than satisfying it. That inversion is the substance of D-66 and is easy to lose once the prose reads as settled.

**Recorded here and not resolved when written.** Removing `aborted` (D-40) and requiring one `onEnd` per started operation (owner final §4) are jointly unsatisfiable against a rule this contract already carries, and the collision is only visible once the callbacks are one.

02 §Landing (D-16) stated: **"the terminal callback is skipped after a consequential failure."** Its reason is sound — the committed frame still says `OUTCOME_ACCEPTED`, so publishing a terminal would announce a successful drop that the queued checkpoint is about to report as failed. Review 3 §11 saw the same gap and answered it with a fifth arm: `onError(error)` then `onEnd({ type: 'aborted' })`. Owner final §4 declined the fifth arm. **Nothing was put in its place**, and with the two callbacks collapsed into one there is nowhere left for the case to hide.

The gap is narrower than it first reads, and the narrowing matters:

- a **non-consequential** classified failure already publishes both channels — that is D-60, and it is settled;
- a consequential failure **before any domain result exists** could honestly publish `canceled`: the drag was abandoned, which is what D-40 says `canceled` means;
- a consequential failure **after the authored commit has already happened** is the hard case. The consumer's data is reordered, the DOM is committed, and the operation reports through `onError` **only**. Under the owner's pairing that is a missing terminal; under 02's rule, publishing `accepted` would be a lie and publishing `canceled` a worse one, since the reorder is real and permanent.

Three ways out, none taken here: reinstate `aborted` (the owner declined it, but declined it against a five-arm _domain_ taxonomy rather than against this case); narrow the pairing invariant to _"unless a consequential failure occurred"_ and accept that `onError` is then the sole terminal signal for it; or publish the domain result the frame actually holds and let `onError` carry the fault, which makes `onEnd` a report of _what happened to the data_ rather than _how the operation ended_.

**The third is what I would choose** — it is the only one that keeps the pairing the owner wrote, it needs no new arm, and it matches what the consumer must actually do (the reorder committed; they have to know). It also inverts 02's stated reason rather than satisfying it, which is why it is a decision for the owner and not a reconciliation.

**The owner took the third, with the tie-break the analysis above left implicit**: _existing result wins, otherwise `canceled`_. That clause is what makes the third option total rather than merely preferable — case 2 and case 3 stop being separate rules and become one lookup on the frame. **D-66** records it. Q-14 is closed in [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md)'s resolved table.

### Revision 2.2 — the kernel vocabulary (D-68)

One decision, closing D-47 §11's queued _minimize the kernel vocabulary_ work, [`ledger.md`](../ledger.md) §Open questions 5 and the half of L-14 that was deferred rather than answered.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-68 | **The kernel tier publishes the structural closure of `BehaviorFactory`, plus every value the SPI hands a behavior whose domain it could not otherwise name — and nothing else.** Added to `kernel.js`: the three `LIFT_*` values and `LiftMode`; the five `SETTLED_*` values; `AT_PROPOSAL`/`AT_CONSUMER` and `CancelStage`; the eight phase values and `Phase`; `toDraggableError`; and the type closure — `Draft`, `Frame`, `KernelFrame`, `OperationIdentity`, `FramePartOf`, `Transition`, `ReleaseTransition`, `SettlementTransition`, `ActionTransition`, `BehaviorInstall`, `LifetimeScope`, `Disposer`, `VisualLiftSession`, `OffsetBox`, `LandingStart`, `LandingContext`, `LandingHandle`. **Four of those are re-homed rather than added** — `Disposer` and the three landing types are published at `sortable/feature.js` today, and `CancelStage`/`AT_*` at `sortable.js`; each keeps that publication as a **re-export** while its declaration site becomes the tier that owns it. **`KernelFrame.phase` narrows from `number` to `Phase`.** Normative surface: [02](02-kernel-behavior-contract.md) §The kernel tier's public vocabulary. | **The tier was not authorable from its own entry, and not marginally** (F-59). `BehaviorConfig.liftMode` requires a `LIFT_*` **value** and `settlement.prepare` requires the `SETTLED_*` values to discriminate the input D-66 travels on; `kernel.js` publishes neither and no path under `kernel/` is a declared export, so the entry could _describe_ a behavior and not _construct_ one. That is stronger than L-14, which recorded the tier as writable inline and not by annotation — the values make it writable in **no** style. **Minimality is a property of the declarations, not of the export list**, which is D-61's rule at the other rung and `sortable/feature.js`'s own stated practice: publishing a type publishes what it structurally names, so the only ways to shrink the surface are to shrink `BehaviorSpec` or to accept its closure. This accepts it, and shrinks nothing — every candidate for elimination (`Disposer`, `FramePartOf`, `BehaviorInstall`, `KernelFrame`) is a name a behavior of the sortable's size writes out of line. **What is deliberately _not_ published is the other half of the decision**: the seam driver, the full `Lifetime`, the frame helpers, the reporter, the invalidation utilities, the protocol event names and the D-46 input-policy helpers. The discriminating rule is _the kernel never hands one to a behavior and never accepts one from it_ — mechanical export of everything internal would have published all seven groups. **Cost, stated:** thirty-two names that were public nowhere become semver surface, including the eight-phase vocabulary D-14 has carried verbatim since probe 1. **Runtime cost is zero** — every added type is erased, every added value is a numeric constant already in the graph, the entry count stays at eight, and `sortable.js`'s bytes do not move. | closes D-47 §11's queued vocabulary pass, ledger §Open questions 5 and L-14's deferred half; corrects 03 §Internal to the ordinary tier, published at the kernel tier; empties `typedoc.json`'s `intentionallyNotExported` |

#### D-68 §Published is not must-name

The two counts are different and only one of them is a usability number.

| Authoring style | Names an author writes |
| --- | --- |
| One factory, seams inline in the object literal | **3–8** — `draggable`, one `LIFT_*`, and the `SETTLED_*` arms it handles. Every parameter is contextually typed. |
| Seams hoisted into their own modules — the sortable's shape | **~30**, which is what `src/sortable/*` imports from `src/kernel/*` today, less the four internal groups |

The published list is 66 names and neither number is 66. That gap is the point: the closure exists so the second style is **possible**, not so anyone types it.

#### D-68 §What self-contained means, and what it does not settle

**Self-contained = `kernel.js` and `drag.js`, with no deep path and no import from another tier.** `drag.js` is not a tier — 03 §The public/internal boundary has it spanning all three — so a behavior author reaching it is reaching sideways, not down. Reaching `sortable.js` or `sortable/feature.js` **is** the inversion D-48 and D-64 exist to prevent, and it is what the tier does today for `Disposer`, `CancelStage` and the three landing types.

**This decision is neutral on whether `drag.js` survives** (handoff §6.5, [`measurements/error-identity.md`](../measurements/error-identity.md)). It adds one data point and takes no position: the kernel tier now names `DraggableError` twice over — as `toDraggableError`'s return and in a behavior's own `instanceof` — which is the same both-tiers-name-it argument the shared root already rests on, neither strengthened nor weakened. If the owner applies the measurement's recommendation, `kernel.js` re-exports the three shared names and the tier becomes one specifier; the vocabulary table below is unchanged either way.

### Phase 18 — free drag (D-69…D-77)

The second behavior's public contract and decomposition. Normative surface: [07](07-free-drag-contract.md). Nothing here reopens Revision 2; **two rows change the kernel**, and both are corrections the second behavior forced rather than preferences it expressed — D-74 because the kernel classifies generic seams with one behavior's vocabulary, D-76 because two Phase 14 decisions are normative and unimplemented.

**D-77 arrived at the phase's closure and changes the behavior that already ships.** It is the only row here that does, and it is the answer to F-67 rather than a free-drag preference: the second behavior made the package's validation rule visibly true of one behavior and false of the other, so the rule was re-derived across both. It **amends D-69's signature**, which no other row does.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-69 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 19).** Signature amended by D-77. **`freeDrag(item, config, ...fragments): FreeDragController`, from a new `free-drag.js`, in one vocabulary — `drag`.** The first argument is the ingress root, which for a free drag _is_ the item; the second is a **complete** `FreeDragConfig` (D-77); every later argument is a plain partial config merged by slot; the return is the controller. The terminal is one `onEnd(result)` over **three** arms — `accepted`, `rejected`, `canceled` — plus an orthogonal `onError`. | Member for member the shape D-48 gave `sortable()`, so the ordinary tier has one form rather than two. **The arm set is re-derived, not inherited**, which the ledger required: the sortable's fourth arm exists because a reorder can be structurally identity-preserving — `from === to` is a real, resolved, successful transaction that changed nothing — and a free drag has no such state, so `noop` would be an arm nothing can produce. The shipped package mixes `FreeDrop*` types with `FreeDrag*` export-site renames; the drop is an event inside the drag, and the drag is what is configured, controlled and named. | ledger §6's deferred decomposition; the `FreeDrop*`/`FreeDrag*` split |
| D-70 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 19).** **`axis`, `lift`, `threshold`, `home` and the callbacks are plain config keys; `bounds` and `landing` are capability installers on their own subpaths.** The contribution is three slots — `constrain`, `startLanding`, `retire` — and `constrain` is a **paired** capability (`apply` + `invalidate` + `retire`) filling a **single-writer** slot. Four new entries: `free-drag.js`, `free-drag/feature.js`, `free-drag/bounds.js`, `free-drag/landing.js`. | D-56's rule — a capability that installs nothing is a config key — applied to the question _does this slot own state or code a composition without it should not carry?_ `axis` is two comparisons and no state, so a factory for it would ship a module and a closure to save two branches. `bounds` owns a resolved rect, a staleness rule and a re-read source, so an unconstrained drag must not carry the resolver or the clamp — which is the test `plan.md` §Phase 18 sets for the composition model. Pairing follows 03 §Geometry is a paired capability for its stated reason and not by analogy: the behavior owns the events that make the rect stale and the feature owns the rect. **Single-writer buys the extensibility story** — grid snapping or magnetic guides fill the same slot _instead of_ `bounds()`, and the library ships none of it. **Cost, stated:** a composition with `bounds()` makes it four post-`MOVE` indirect calls rather than three; one without pays a property read and a predictable branch. | ledger §6's "features and subpaths deferred to Phase 18" |
| D-71 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 19).** **Every mutable policy slot is a source the library re-reads, `invalidate()` is the only signal, and `moveTo(point)` is a separate motion command. There is no `update()`.** The controller is four members: `invalidate`, `moveTo`, `cancel`, `destroy`. `moveTo` **re-bases** — the visual is at `point` on the next committed frame and later pointer motion continues relative to that. | D-44 generalized from the collection to policy. `updateItems` was deleted on the finding that the package carried **two channels for one thing and re-read neither**; `update({ bounds })` beside a `bounds` source is the identical shape at a different slot. `invalidate()` carries no payload and is applied as a **queued action**, so consumer sources are read inside `action.prepare` where a transaction is open and a throw has a stage — reading them on the `invalidate()` statement would run consumer code at an arbitrary reentrant point. **A controlled position is not policy**: folding it into an options bag is what made the shipped `update({ position })` carry a command. **Two real losses, stated rather than respelled**: live `onMove` replacement and live landing timing, both recoverable in two lines by the consumer. | ledger L-5 and the `FreeDragController.update` row |
| D-72 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 19).** **The coordinate space narrows to deltas.** `localDelta` stays and is derived **by default** from box-quad's ancestor linear part; `coordinateSpace`, `CoordinateMapper` and `localPosition` are dropped; every point on the surface — `home`'s return, `moveTo`'s argument, the request's positions — is viewport. `FreeDragHome` collapses into `Point`. | The shipped default mapper is an `offsetParent` walk — a coordinate module this package deleted and Phase 19 forbids re-adding — so the question was never _is a mapper useful_ but _what can be derived without it_. box-quad already hands the lift the inherited ancestor space's **linear part**, which is exactly what a delta needs and is already consumed for the in-place projection; it exposes no translation, which is exactly what a point needs. The decision follows that seam rather than splitting the difference. **The two shipped stories the option existed for — Zoomed Context, Transformed Stage — are ancestor-transform cases, and drag2's port of the first already works with no mapper.** What a consumer loses is an arbitrary non-ancestor space, which it can map itself because it holds both ends. | ledger L-7 and the `coordinateSpace`/`CoordinateMapper` rows |
| D-73 | **Superseded 2026-08-26 by D-141**, which deletes the string domain and hands the slot the kernel's own `LiftMode`. ~~**Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 19).** **Lift modes are a consumer domain of three strings, mapped by the behavior**: `'faithful' \| 'flat' \| 'in-place'`, default `'faithful'`. The kernel's `LIFT_*` enum stays kernel-tier. | The ledger's open question 2 asked whether a behavior may expose a kernel-internal enum. It may not and need not: D-68 publishes the enum for a **kernel-tier author** who must fill `BehaviorConfig.liftMode`, and the ordinary tier gets its own domain — which is D-47's progressive disclosure working rather than the inversion it exists to prevent. **The strings are renamed from the shipped ones and it is breaking**: both promoted modes use the top layer in this package, so `'top-layer'` names one of them after a mechanism it shares with its sibling, and `'none'` says _no lift_ for a mode that lifts, suppresses transitions and projects coordinates. Precedent is `vertical()` → `y()` — a layout word for a rule about a coordinate — and the migration is a three-row table.~~ **What dates it is its own premise**: it declined to hand an ordinary consumer a kernel-internal enum, and D-68 had already published that enum — so the strings bought a second name for each mode rather than an encapsulation. | ledger §Open questions 2 and the `lift` row |
| D-74 | **Three failure stages are renamed and no numeric value changes**: `FAILURE_INSERTION` → **`FAILURE_ACTION_PREPARE`** (4), `FAILURE_PLACEHOLDER_MOVE` → **`FAILURE_ACTION_EFFECT`** (5), `FAILURE_REORDER_RESOLUTION` → **`FAILURE_RESOLUTION`** (8). | **The kernel classifies two behavior-generic seams with one behavior's vocabulary** (F-62), and not as a default a behavior may override: the settlement seam runs under `FAILURE_REORDER_RESOLUTION` and the action seam under `FAILURE_INSERTION`/`FAILURE_PLACEHOLDER_MOVE` for **every** behavior. So a free-drag `moveTo()` whose prepare throws reports an _insertion_ failure, mapped by D-64 to a **presentation** fault, for library arithmetic. Checkpoint E asks whether anything in `kernel/` knows an insertion or a placeholder; three published constants do. **No SPI change, no wire change, and no consumer is affected** — D-64 gives consumers a coarse `code`, never a stage — so the only readers are kernel-tier authors, of whom there are none yet. That window is why this is now or never: D-68 made these names permanent semver surface one revision ago. The rejected alternative was behavior-supplied action stages, which widens `BehaviorConfig` for every behavior to fix a naming problem. | F-62; the stage names D-64 moved to `kernel.js` and D-68 published |
| D-75 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 19).** **A name is qualified when two entries need different structures under one word, and only then.** `FreeDragLift` (against `kernel.js`'s numeric `LiftMode`) and the symmetric pair `SortableErrorContext`/`FreeDragErrorContext` (against one `DragErrorContext` carrying a behavior's own result). `DragAxis` and `DragGeometry` stay unqualified. | Two structurally different types under one name is the identity hazard F-61 found inside the package, arriving from the outside. **The sortable's rename is included deliberately**: leaving `DragErrorContext` where it is would give the first behavior the unqualified word by arrival order, and the package has no released consumer, so symmetry costs one type name now and cannot be had later. The rule is kept narrow on purpose — qualifying every name a second behavior _could_ want would rename half the surface for a collision that does not exist. | 03 §The export topology's `DragErrorContext` cell |
| D-76 | **Landed 2026-08-15.** **D-34 and D-35 land as a discrete kernel step before Phase 19**, against the sortable alone, with zero free-drag code in the tree: the parameterized activation staged type threaded through the construction generics, the lift session recording what `write` composed, `LandingContext.from` reading it, the `BehaviorLiftSession` projection, 05's D-35 matrix group executing, and M-1/M-3 re-measured for the two scalar writes. | **Both are contract-normative and unimplemented** (F-63), and free drag is not merely their first consumer — it is incorrect without them. It stages nothing at activation, so under the shipped signature its `prepare` returns an element it does not own and its `effect` ignores it (F-44 exactly); it constrains its visual on three independent paths, so under the shipped computation every constrained drop opens its landing from a position the visual has never been at (F-45 exactly, and Phase 11 found one of that shape the hard way). **The sequencing is the decision, not the content.** D-35 changes the hot path and re-verifies the landing for the behavior that already ships; landing it beside a new behavior gives any regression two candidate causes and moves the sortable's numbers under a phase that is not measuring them. | `plan.md` §Phase 14's booking of D-34/D-35 to "Phases 19–20" |
| D-77 | **Implemented for the sortable, 2026-08-15**, as the discrete step before Phase 19; `freeDrag()`'s half lands with the behavior itself, under D-69's row. **Required configuration is a required first argument; every later fragment stays `Partial`.** `sortable(root, config: SortableConfig, ...fragments: ReadonlyArray<Partial<SortableConfig>>)` and `freeDrag(item, config: FreeDragConfig, ...fragments: ReadonlyArray<Partial<FreeDragConfig>>)` — **amending D-69's signature**. `y()` and `xy()` return the installer itself, so an axis is written `axis: y()` inside that first argument. The package-wide rule 03 §Public option domains froze is replaced by: **a construction-time throw is permitted only for an invariant over what installers _contribute_** — required configuration is a type obligation, and a consumer scalar's domain belongs to the consumer, the platform, or the seam that consumes it. Five explicit sortable checks go (`axis` missing, `items`/`onReorder` not functions, the axis installer's missing geometry, `threshold`'s domain); `claim`'s single-writer collision stays; `copyUniqueItems` stays and is classified; the landing duration narrows to `=== Infinity`. Sortable construction **diagnostics: 6 → 1** — a count of explicit checks, not of failures: deleting the axis checks deletes their _messages_, and a JS-authored violator still meets a native `TypeError` from the dereference underneath (03 §Validation carries the per-slot table). Free drag's `onDrop` becomes a compile error instead of a first-release `FAILURE_RESOLUTION`. | **F-67, re-derived across both behaviors as one decision.** §1.2 decides it: a missing required slot _is_ expressible at compile time, so paying runtime bytes for it is exactly the rule that section names. **The form matters more than the verdict.** 03 §Fragment factories already asked whether the missing case could be a compile error and answered no — but it examined only a **variadic tuple-merge type**, which it correctly bounded on two failures: a spread call has no tuple to fold, and a `Partial<SortableConfig>` variable erases the literal's key set, so the check silently passes for the one input it exists to catch. **A required first parameter has neither failure**, because it folds nothing and a `Partial` value is not assignable to it. The third option was never considered, and it is strictly sound where the second was unsound. **What is lost, named:** a later fragment can no longer supply a required slot, so a preset that carries one must declare it (`Pick<SortableConfig, 'axis'> & Partial<SortableConfig>`) and be spread into the first argument — one line, still type-checked. **The residual runtime hole is stated rather than patched:** a consumer who writes `{ items, ...spread }` where the spread carries `items: undefined` merges to nothing — and it does **not** reach a seam. It fails at the **construction pull**, where `sortable()` calls `items()` before returning, with the consumer's own native `TypeError` and no classification, no coarse code and no `onError`. ~~fails at the first drag, and is diagnosed by the seam~~ named a route that does not exist: admission reads the prebuilt snapshot, and the only in-seam pull is `action.prepare(COLLECTION)`, which `controller.invalidate()` alone dispatches — so nothing gets as far as a drag. The verdict §1.1 gives it is unchanged and is what the row rests on: "the consumer would only break their own code". **`y()`/`xy()` unwrap because the wrapper's only purpose was fragment position**, which required slots no longer have; the alternative kept `Pick<'axis'>` and made the natural spelling `axis: y()` a type error while the correct one was a spread (§9, §12). | F-67; 03 §Public option domains; 03 §Fragment factories' open question; the D-69 signature |

### The D-77 landing review (D-78…D-80)

The review of D-77's landing ([`../reviews/phase-18-after-review.md`](../reviews/phase-18-after-review.md)) found the implementation correct and the documentation around it not. Of its twenty findings, most are stale prose the commit did not carry. **Three needed a decision rather than an edit**, and they are below. **None reopens D-77's implemented behavior** — no seam, no signature and no runtime outcome moves — which is the constraint the review was read under, and D-80 is the only row that touches `src/` at all.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-78 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16.** **A public type's closure resolves within its own tier plus the tiers below it, and `AxisInstaller` is published at the ordinary tier.** The ordinary tier closes over `sortable.js ∪ drag.js ∪ sortable/feature.js`; the kernel tier's `kernel.js ∪ drag.js` is unchanged (D-68). `AxisInstaller` is re-exported from `sortable.js` because it names a slot of `SortableConfig`; its own closure — `FeatureContext`, `SortableContribution`, `InsertionGeometry` and everything those name — stays declared at `sortable/feature.js`. **The opacity claim is retracted at all three sites** (03 §The schema, `src/sortable/feature.ts`, `src/sortable/config.ts`): a `sortable.js`-only consumer **can** write `axis: (ctx) => ({ … })`, because contextual typing resolves a parameter's type structurally whether or not its alias is re-exported. `tests/docs.node.test.ts` gains a per-entry run over the ordinary tier's union, beside the kernel tier's. **The two obligations are separate instruments and neither substitutes for the other**: the per-tier TypeDoc run proves only that a public type's closure stays **within the allowed tier union** — it is satisfied whether or not `sortable.js` re-exports `AxisInstaller`, because `sortable/feature.js` is _in_ that union, verified by deleting the re-export and watching it stay green; **publication is a hoistability property**, and it is pinned by the packed ordinary-consumer fixture, which hoists `const hoistedAxis: AxisInstaller` importing `sortable.js` alone and fails to compile without the re-export. | **P18A-04 and P18A-05, and the first was disproved by compiling rather than by reading** — a file importing only `src/sortable.ts` typechecks clean against the very shape 03 says it cannot express. **The naive repair is worse than the defect**: `AxisInstaller`'s closure is substantially the whole of `sortable/feature.js`, so applying _every alias it names_ transitively would publish the middle tier at the ordinary one and dissolve D-61. **Progressive disclosure governs discoverability and reusable authoring vocabulary, not prohibition** — a tier decides where a name is _declared_; it never decides what the compiler will let a consumer write, and it does not by itself decide what they can hoist either, since the hoistable name can be — and here is — published at their own tier. An ordinary-tier consumer may author an installer **inline** _and_ hoist it as `AxisInstaller`; what `sortable/feature.js` buys is the **lower-level named authoring vocabulary** for an installer's parts, not the ability to construct the shape. Claiming otherwise made a property of the entry map out of a property of neither. So the closure rule becomes tier-scoped, which is the `kernel.js ∪ drag.js` precedent applied at the tier that never had one, and D-61's rung survives intact. **Not a freeze-rule event**: an addition to the public consumer surface, which ground rule 5 and §Status both put outside the rule — the same class as D-68. **Stated behavior-neutrally on purpose**: free drag's config carries installer-typed slots and its own middle tier (F-64), and a rule written as a sortable fact gets re-derived per behavior, which is exactly how F-67 happened. | 03 §The schema's opacity sentence; `src/sortable.ts`'s _every alias it names_; the whole-run docs check as the ordinary tier's only closure instrument |
| D-79 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16.** **The `animate()` domain gets an artifact under [`../measurements/`](../measurements/), and its justification narrows to what the call path can reach.** The measurement pins the two load-bearing clauses and only those: `animate()` **rejects** `NaN`, negatives, `-Infinity`, strings and objects, and **accepts `Infinity` and never completes** — which is why the surviving guard is `=== Infinity` and not `Number.isFinite`. **`undefined` is struck from the claim at all five sites**, and `'auto'` is marked reachable from JavaScript only. | **P18A-08 and P18A-09.** Five sites carry the claim — `src/sortable/slots.ts`, `src/sortable/landing.ts`, the README, 03 and 07 — as the sole justification for _deleting_ `requireFinite` rather than relocating it, and **nothing in the tree records it**: no measurement, no probe, and no test of any clause. That is the evidence type this package otherwise refuses — `../measurements/` exists for exactly it, D-76's gate demanded re-measurement before it could land, and `tests/decisions.node.test.ts` exists because a green suite is evidence about the _implemented_ contract only. **The verdict is unaffected and rests on two legs, neither of them the falsified one**: the byte argument (`CODE_OF_SIZE.md` §1.3) and the `Infinity` invariant, which is a library-liveness concern no platform check covers. **What fails is the third leg** — _a finiteness test would have wrongly refused values the platform accepts_ is not a property of this code, because `src/sortable/landing.ts` coalesces `duration` to its default before `animate()` can ever see `undefined`. A reason that survives only as a JS-only string must not be carried in five places as though it were the argument. | the unrecorded Chrome 150 claim at its five sites; F-67's _accepts `'auto'` and `undefined`_ clause |
| D-80 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16.** **Two statements are scoped to what holds, and the collection is validated before the first installer runs.** **(a)** The `AxisInstaller` backstop covers **presence, not well-formedness** — `{ insertion: {} }` passes assembly and surfaces later at the seam — so 03 §Validation and `src/sortable/feature.ts` state the pairing as _the type is total for a TypeScript consumer; the runtime dereference exists for a JavaScript one, and checks that the object exists_. **(b)** **`copyUniqueItems` moves ahead of `assemble`**: the collection is pulled, validated and copied once, before any installer runs, and the validated copy is passed into `install`. _The unwind is total_ then holds across construction rather than within `assemble`, and needs no scope clause. | **P18A-12 and P18A-13 — and the second is rated a scope note by the review that found it, which understates it (F-68).** `copyUniqueItems` throws a `TypeError` on a duplicate element from `src/sortable/runtime.ts`, inside `createSortableRuntime`, i.e. **after `assemble` has returned**: a consumer whose `items()` yields the same element twice leaves every installer's `retire` hook collected and never run, plus a kernel and a realm `draggable()` has already built and nothing destroys, because `arm()` is never reached. Consumer-triggerable, and the shape is worth keeping: **D-77 retained exactly one construction-time throw, and it sits in the one window the unwind does not cover.** **Making the sentence true is cheaper than scoping it** — a reordering, no bracket machinery, no new state — and it is safe precisely because **installers are externally inert by contract** (03 §A feature is a function factory: an installer may allocate, but may not attach a listener, write the DOM, or acquire anything needing release), so running them after the validation is not externally observable. **This does not reopen D-77**: `copyUniqueItems` stays and is still classified at its other call site, and the throw's type, message and destination are unchanged — only its position moves. **The opposite move from D-37, deliberately**: that row narrowed a domain because the guarantee could not be checked, and here it can be made true for free, so narrowing would be the lazy reading of the precedent. | 03 §Validation's backstop sentence and its `src/sortable/feature.ts` twin; the unstated scope on _the unwind is total_ in 03 §Validation and [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md) |

### The Phase 19 contradictions (D-81, D-82)

Phase 19 landed green and raised two contradictions rather than designing around them. Both are **claim-side**: the shipped behavior is the intended one in each case, and what disagrees with it is a sentence written before the behavior existed. **Neither decision changes free drag's design or the kernel SPI.**

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-81 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Phase 20).** Its contract half landed with the decision and its source half landed here: `src/free-drag/bounds.ts` now names all four seams and states that `FAILURE_ACTION_PREPARE` is unreachable from this row, so the deferred table's witness stopped holding and that row retired with it. **The bounds source's failure attribution is _the seam that next renders_, and the contract says so instead of naming one stage.** The feature resolves lazily on the first `apply` after a staleness mark; `invalidate()` is a staleness flag that calls nothing. A garbage source therefore surfaces at `activation.effect` (`FAILURE_ACTIVATION` → `interaction`) on its **first** resolve, and thereafter at `moved` (`FAILURE_RENDERER_WRITE`) or a `TAG_POSITION` effect (`FAILURE_ACTION_EFFECT`), both `presentation`, or at `release.prepare` (`FAILURE_RELEASE` → `interaction`). **`FAILURE_ACTION_PREPARE` is not reachable from this row at all.** 07 §Validation, B-4 (b), L-3, L-4 and [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md)'s free-drag group are corrected, and **Phase 20 asserts all four paths plus the negative** — `tests/free-drag/validation.browser.test.ts` §a garbage bounds source, whose last row is that nothing surfaces from the action that marks the rect stale. | **F-71, and the stronger attribution is not semantically required.** A stage answers _where the library was standing_, not _whose data was bad_ — the reading 07's own D-74 note already takes when it declines to defend `presentation` for library arithmetic, and the reading this column's header (_where a bad value surfaces_) states outright. Under it the shipped attribution is correct and the sentence is what is wrong. **The two ways to restore a single stage both buy documentation with runtime**: resolving eagerly inside `invalidate()` puts a layout read or a consumer call on **every scroll and resize event**, which is the one path this package refuses to load; re-raising through a third behavior action contradicts 07's `actionTags: 2`; and a fourth `MotionConstraint` member widens a contract type to move a label. **F-71 understated itself twice and the corrections matter more than the finding** — F-73 (activation is the first resolve, so the dominant path for a bad source was named by neither the finding nor the code comment) and F-74 (the two-consumer-call barrier is not instantiated by the first-party `bounds()`, so L-3's fixture cannot discriminate as written). **What stays open is the general question, not this row**: whether a stage is a promise or a description is Checkpoint E's, and D-81 deliberately answers it only for the width of one table cell. | 07 §Validation's `action.prepare` cell; B-4 (b)'s `presentation` for `bounds`; L-3's _two-consumer-call_ premise; 05's matching clause |
| D-82 | **The duplicate activation-time box-quad traversal is deferred to Checkpoint E, beside F-65, and the SPI does not move in Phase 19 or 20.** `release.effect`'s literal _nothing_ is separately withdrawn: free drag writes the release delta through `lift.write`, F-39 applied to a behavior with no placeholder, and **unbranched** because free drag has no `command` and therefore no pointerless operation. | **F-72, and the freeze rule already decides the first half.** Only a failing executable case reopens the SPI, and one extra traversal per activation — on a path already doing layout work, never per sample, with the coefficients captured once — is not one; it is a measurement Phase 21 owes. **The pairing is the reason to defer rather than to close**: F-65 says the kernel measures something only the sortable needs and F-72 says it withholds something free drag needs from the same traversal, so they are one question about what `ActivationScope` carries, and answering half of it under a phase that is not measuring either would set the SPI by whichever behavior asked last. **The `release.effect` half is not a deferral and must not travel with it** — it is a live contradiction between a normative table cell and shipped, correct code, and F-39 makes the write normative rather than incidental (02 §The command destination, 05 §F-39). Leaving _nothing_ standing would license a later pass to delete the write and pass the contract. | 07's `release.effect` row |

### The Phase 20 mismatches (D-83, D-84)

Phase 20's executable matrix drove the rows that Phase 19's prose only described, and two claims did not survive contact. Both are **claim-side**, both are corrections to sentences rather than to seams, and **neither behavior changes**.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-83 | **The terminal guarantee is scoped to the observable-start boundary, and per-behavior documents cite [05](05-lifecycle-invariants.md) I-31 rather than restating it.** A classified failure **before** `onStart` — the progress marker still at `MINTED` — publishes `onError` and **no terminal**; a classified failure **after** it publishes **exactly one** on a still-live controller. Applied to 07's B-4 (b) and 05's free-drag group, which now assert the terminal per row against the boundary instead of once for the table. **No invariant changes and no machinery is added**: I-31 and D-66 §The progress marker already say exactly this. | **F-75, and the interesting part is that the rule was already right.** I-31 has read _if a start is notified **and the controller remains alive**, exactly one terminal follows — **and only then**_ since Revision 2.1, and it is stated behavior-neutrally. What failed is a **restatement**: B-4 (b) re-expressed it as a property of a validation table, and a table has no antecedent to carry, so the condition was dropped in the copy. Three rows — `handle`, `visual` and `bounds` on its first resolve — then asserted a terminal that D-66 forbids, and Q-15 forbids for the same reason a press that never activates publishes none. **The scoping is therefore not a concession to free drag** and must not be written as one: patching only 07 would leave the sortable's inherited phrasing free to drift the same way, which is why the durable half of this row is the prohibition on restating I-31 at all. **The executable matrix is what made it undeniable** — the rows were asserted as observed, so the contradiction arrived as a passing test against a contract that says otherwise, which is the one form these documents cannot argue with. | 07 B-4 (b)'s table-wide _exactly one terminal_; 05's free-drag restatement of it |
| D-84 | **A throwing `visual` resolver is `FAILURE_ADMISSION` (1) → `consumer`, in both behaviors**, and `visual` is no longer paired with `onStart` in any row. Free drag calls the resolver inside `admit`; the sortable calls it inside `seedDraft`, admission's second half. `onStart` keeps `FAILURE_ACTIVATION` (2) → `interaction`. 07 §Validation splits the row, and B-4 (b) and 05's group are corrected with it. | **F-76, and the derivation error is the reusable part.** The row's stage was taken from the kernel lines that _name_ the stage rather than from the site that _calls the resolver_ — which is **exactly** how D-81's `bounds` row went wrong one decision earlier, from a different starting point. Two instances make it a pattern worth naming: **an attribution is a fact about a call site, and this table's column header says so**; reading it off a stage constant's neighbourhood produces a plausible answer that is wrong by one seam. **`consumer` is also the better attribution on the merits**, so nothing is traded for accuracy here: the visual resolver is consumer code, it runs before any operation identity exists, and `interaction` would tell a consumer their pointer interaction failed when their own function threw. **The pairing was the mechanism of the error** — two slots sharing one row cannot carry two stages, so the moment they diverged the row had to be wrong about one of them, and it was wrong about the one that moved earliest. | 07 §Validation's `visual`, `onStart` row; B-4 (b)'s `interaction` for `visual`; 05's matching clause |

### Checkpoint E's blockers (D-85…D-87)

The [Checkpoint E review](../reviews/checkpoint-e/review-checkpoint-e-1-codex.md) raised six blockers. **Three need a decision and are below; three are implementation fixes under contracts that already say what to do**, and saying so is itself the disposition rather than a deferral:

- **E-02** — the activation terminal barrier is claimed at `:410-420` and implemented before the last contributed call in both behaviors. **I-36 already requires the read where the review puts it**: after the last consumer-reachable or third-party call, before the next write, publication, progress advance or `onStart`. No new liveness SPI, no shared extraction — the two fixes having the same shape is not evidence of a common mechanism, and D-53's `host.closed` is already in both behaviors.
- **E-03** — `reportFailure` after `destroy()`. **I-31's `destroy()` exception and D-53's logical-closure latch already forbid it**; the guard belongs at the two kernel-owned call sites because the rule is controller lifetime, not domain settlement, and both behaviors reach them identically.
- **E-05** — an invalid `home` result escaping its own quality seam. **D-49 already attributes it to `FAILURE_LANDING_TARGET` on the quality track**; validating and copying inside `anchorTarget` is what makes the shipped code match the attribution (07 §Validation) already publishes. Kernel-side point validation is refused: only free drag crosses a consumer-result boundary here, and the sortable's anchor is behavior-owned DOM geometry.

**F-65 is unchanged and stays booked for Phase 21 measurement.**

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-85 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Checkpoint E remediation).** **`ActivationScope` carries `inheritedSpace`: the inverse inherited linear part, or `null`, derived from the measurement `acquireLift` already took before it mutated anything.** The behavior multiplies; it never measures. `BehaviorLiftSession` is unchanged, no `Box` crosses the seam, and `captureLocalSpace` and its four private index constants are deleted with the second traversal. `InheritedSpace` publishes at `kernel.js` as part of the scope's closure (D-68). | **E-01, and it is a correctness fix rather than the overhead item F-72 filed it as.** The second traversal runs **after** acquisition has changed positioning, dimensions, top-layer state and transforms, so a single activation could lift on one coordinate snapshot and report consumer deltas from another; box-quad's own contract says a second walk may legitimately disagree. **The two reads also disagreed on failure** — `acquireLift` throws `FAILURE_ACTIVATION` for an unreadable space while `captureLocalSpace` silently substituted identity — and deriving from the one successful measurement leaves nothing to disagree with. **Placement is against the review's suggestion and the reasons are in [02](02-kernel-behavior-contract.md) §Why `inheritedSpace` is on the scope**: the session is a positive `Pick` describing post-lift state, and it already holds a same-shaped projection with a **different value** — `null` for both lifted modes — so reusing it would hand free drag the identity under `LIFT_FLAT`, wrong silently. **The tension with F-65 is real and is not hidden**: this adds unconditional kernel work for a need only one behavior has, which is F-65's own complaint pointed the other way. It is accepted on two grounds that F-65 does not have — the marginal cost is arithmetic over a buffer already materialized rather than a **layout read**, and it buys correctness rather than bytes. Phase 21 measures it beside F-65. | F-72's Checkpoint E deferral (D-82), which read this as measurable overhead |
| D-86 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Checkpoint E remediation).** **Free-drag action legality is defined per tag: `TAG_POSITION` and `TAG_POLICY` are legal in `ACTIVATING` and `ACTIVE`, and are deterministic no-ops after.** `prepare` returns the seam's existing `null` discard on a late tag — no failure, no report, no terminal. The `host.closed` guards stay; they answer a different question. | **E-04, and the two tags are defined separately because their reasons do not travel together.** `TAG_POSITION` is refused for **correctness**: from `onEnd` it is FIFO-ahead of `RETIRE` and writes through an already-disposed lift, which the review's probe caught as a stray inline transform on a released element. `TAG_POLICY` is refused for **hygiene**: it writes no geometry and re-enters declared consumer and third-party slots for no observable effect once no later sample exists. The sets coincide today only because free drag takes no sample after release. **`ACTIVATING` is included deliberately, not incidentally** — a `moveTo()` from `onStart` is intentional retargeting, and the queue may drain it in either of the two live phases. **Not solved in the kernel**, and the wrong fix is instructive: filtering behavior tags by phase in the router would break the sortable, which **intentionally** accepts a collection `invalidate()` in phases where free-drag geometry must be frozen — a collection change during settlement is real information and a position write is not. Behavior action legality is behavior knowledge, which is why the kernel routes tags without interpreting them. | 07's action rows, which stated no phase legality at all |
| D-87 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (Checkpoint E remediation).** **Cross-behavior installers are unsupported and non-assignable, enforced by one optional `never` slot per direction**: `FreeDragContribution` declares `insertion?: never`, `SortableContribution` declares `constrain?: never`. Negative declaration tests both ways, `AxisInstaller → FreeDragInstaller` named explicitly. | **E-06, and the mechanism is chosen to state the incompatibility rather than to mark it.** Every real slot on both records is optional, so they were mutually assignable and free drag's `plugins` accepted an `AxisInstaller` whose `insertion` the assembler then **silently discarded** — a supported middle-tier API accepting a value and doing nothing with it. **One exclusion per direction is the minimum that works**, because assignability is decided on the declared alias rather than on a particular body, so it also refuses the required-`insertion` case. **A runtime brand and a phantom discriminant are both refused**: each costs a field or a name and says only _these are different_, while `insertion?: never` says the true thing — a free-drag contribution has no insertion, and an object carrying one is not one. **An installer returning `{}` stays assignable to both, correctly**: an empty contribution is genuinely valid for either, and refusing it would be a boundary drawn for its own sake. **The obligation is stated rather than discovered** — a new single-writer slot needs its `never` twin, or the boundary reopens for that slot. At three behaviors that becomes a matrix and an explicit common contribution contract earns its keep; at two it does not, which is F-64's judgement unchanged. | the mutual assignability of `SortableInstaller`, `AxisInstaller` and `FreeDragInstaller` |

### The second Checkpoint E audit (D-88…D-91)

An [independent audit](../reviews/checkpoint-e/review-checkpoint-e-1-claude.md) run after the D-85…D-87 remediation found four items needing judgement. **One re-opens a decision this ledger recorded as closed**, which is the row to read first. CE1-05, CE1-06, CE1-07, CE1-08 and CE1-09 are mechanical remediation under contracts that already hold and need no decision; CE1-10 and CE1-11 are dispositioned below the table as Checkpoint E evidence.

| ID | Decision | Why | Supersedes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-88 | **Superseded 2026-08-26 by D-138**, which moves the boundary onto the context as a `unique symbol` brand and deletes every exclusion. The finding below stands unchanged — D-87's sufficiency claim was wrong and the escaped form was real — and what D-138 rejects is the remedy, because key-set totality bought the boundary by making each record enumerate the other behavior's capability names. **The residue this row records as a bound returns with the mechanism**, and D-138 records it as accepted rather than closed. **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (second Checkpoint E remediation).** ~~**The cross-behavior exclusion rule is key-set totality: both contribution records declare the same key set, and a key a behavior does not implement it declares `?: never`.** Free drag adds `placeholder`, `beforeInsertionMove` and `afterInsertionMove` to its existing `insertion`; the sortable's `constrain` already stands. `tests/composition.declaration.test.ts` asserts `keyof` equality between the two records, and each open slot gets an **unannotated** counter-example driven through free drag's public `plugins`.~~ | **CE1-01, and D-87's sufficiency claim was wrong** — _one exclusion per direction is the minimum that works_ closed the two defining capabilities and left three sortable slots open. **What appeared to close them was TypeScript's weak-type detection**, which an all-optional target gets for free and which `retire` — the one member both records genuinely share — defeats. A hoisted **unannotated** installer carrying `{ placeholder, retire }` compiles into `plugins` today and is silently discarded by an assembler reading three slots, which is the exact defect E-06 was raised about, surviving the decision that claimed to close it. **The boundary is asymmetric because the slot sets are**, and _one per direction_ described a symmetry that does not exist. **The maintenance obligation becomes a check rather than a promise**: D-87 wrote the twin rule as future work, and it was already owed for three existing slots — a key-set equality assertion fails at the moment a slot is added without its twin, which is the only form that cannot drift. **The pinning requirement is load-bearing and is stated with the rule**: every row of the existing suite starts from an annotated alias, which is the one form the old exclusions already caught, so a fix leaving the suite unchanged is unpinned exactly where the boundary escaped. **The rule is exact over the seven known keys and structurally cannot refuse an eighth, which is recorded as a bound rather than left to be discovered.** A hoisted unannotated installer carrying an unrecognized slot — `{ onGridSnap, retire }` — typechecks into free drag's `plugins` and is discarded by an assembler reading three slots: the same _a supported middle-tier API taking a value and doing nothing with it_ shape CE1-01 objected to, arriving from a typo or a future slot name rather than from the other behavior's capabilities. **It stays a bound, because nothing in the contract asks for more.** Exactness needs branded or exact contribution types — machinery D-88 deliberately declined, and which F-64/D-61 argue against, since a shared exactness mechanism _is_ the cross-behavior composition vocabulary Checkpoint E is convened to gather evidence for rather than to build. [03](03-feature-composition.md) §Fragments are public, installers are opaque already records that structural typing admits a matching literal whether or not a name is exported, and the middle tier's versioning promise covers the members it **declares**, not the absence of members it does not. **The residue is asymmetric with CE1-01's, and that asymmetry is why it is tolerable**: a foreign _capability_ silently discarded is a supported act producing nothing, while an unknown _key_ is a value no tier ever declared. Recorded so a later reader does not read the `keyof` equality row as a stronger guarantee than it is. | D-87's _one exclusion per direction is the minimum that works_, and its reading of the declaration suite as sufficient evidence |
| D-89 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (second Checkpoint E remediation).** **`anchorTarget`'s accepted arm reads the committed delta rather than re-deriving it**, so it calls no constraint and there is no fifth `constrain.apply` seam. `release.prepare` derives the release delta and `release.effect` writes it; the join reads the same values. **The invariant this rests on is stated and pinned**: nothing may write that delta between `release.prepare` and the join, which D-86 now guarantees by making both behavior tags no-ops after `ACTIVE`. | **CE1-02, and the redundancy is what makes the choice easy.** The accepted arm re-computed the same values from the same committed frame **in order to re-enter third-party code** — the seam's own comment claimed _no consumer call and no DOM read_, the I-36 Category-1 table omitted `constrain.apply` entirely, and D-81's re-derived four-seam enumeration missed it. Removing the re-derivation makes all three true at once, which is why it beats the alternative of declaring a fifth seam and owing it an attribution, a barrier and a table row. **The barrier defect goes with it**: `host.closed` is read immediately before `home` and not before `deriveMotion`, so a third-party `apply` ran after logical closure while the resolver beside it was guarded — a split inside one seam, E-02's shape one seam further on. **Reachability was narrow and that is not the argument**: with the first-party `bounds()` the resolve is a cache hit, and the defect is real for the third-party constraint the middle tier was opened for. **The derivation error repeats D-81's exactly** — a constraint seam reasoned from where staleness is _raised_ rather than from where `apply` is _called_, for the third time. | 07's `anchorTarget` row; the I-36 Category-1 table's omission; D-81's four-seam list |
| D-90 | **Implemented, 2026-08-18 (Checkpoint E closure).** **Re-opened 2026-08-18 and closed the same day; the source half shipped 2026-08-16.** **A `MotionConstraint`'s members are never invoked with that `MotionConstraint` as their receiver; an author may not depend on `this`** — the mechanism half withdrawn by D-93, the referent corrected by D-94. ~~Contribution members are invoked detached. ~~The three call sites~~ **The five sites** (D-93; this stale count corrected by D-94) are consistent with it~~ — **five sites, not three** (D-93; the stale count survived here alone, CE6-02), and _detached_ survives as the convention's **name** rather than as a sentence anything rests on. The contract section landed. ~~The marker cannot express a partial landing, so the split is narrated here~~ — **both halves are now in the tree, so the split it narrated is closed.** The sentence is on `MotionConstraint` itself, where the third-party installer author reading the published type meets it, and `tests/free-drag/anchor.browser.test.ts` drives each call site alone with a constraint whose members record the receiver they are handed — ~~three members reached through four sites~~ — **three members reached through five sites** (D-93): `invalidate` is called from both the scroll/resize listener and `TAG_POLICY`, and `retire` from both the normal retirement and the assembler's construction unwind. **Closure was measured against the recorded reversion rather than against the fixture's shape**: binding the three `spec.ts` sites fails three rows, and binding the assembler's `retire` fails ~~the fourth~~ **the fourth and the fifth** (D-93, measured), each run before this marker was flipped. The `spec.ts` comment that asserted the missing sentence is true now and says so (C-1). | **CE1-03.** The tree **called** the same three-member record both bound and detached, so a `this`-reading constraint **worked** from `controller.invalidate()` and **threw** on the first scroll and at every retirement — the pre-fix state, and the whole reason the convention had to be written rather than assumed. **Detached rather than bound**, for the reason 03 §Assembly already gives for the sortable's identical flattening — one property read and one call at the seam — and because the alternative leaves **two conventions in one package** for the author who writes against both middle tiers. ~~Nothing fails today only because the one shipped constraint closes over its state, which is the non-discriminating control F-74 names, met by the first third-party author instead of by a test.~~ **True for two days, and no longer** (2026-08-18): `bounds()` is still the F-74 control and still proves nothing, but a fixture that records its receiver now fails at whichever site is bound, so the convention is met by the suite rather than by the first third-party author. | the unstated convention at `MotionConstraint`'s ~~three~~ **five** call sites |
| D-91 | **Implemented, 2026-08-16 (second Checkpoint E remediation).** **`moveTo(point)` requires two finite coordinates. A non-finite one discards the action** — `prepare` returns `null`, nothing is written — **and is surfaced on the platform reporter**: no classified failure, no `onError`, no terminal. A malformed `point` is **not** checked and throws naturally at the read, reaching `FAILURE_ACTION_PREPARE` → `presentation`. | **CE1-04, and the asymmetry with E-05 was decided by silence rather than by judgement.** The surface takes two consumer-supplied points; E-05 decided `home` must be read, checked and copied inside its seam, and `moveTo`'s domain appeared in no table at all. **The silent-table precedent does not transfer**: a `NaN` `threshold` is silent because the drag never starts and no operation exists, whereas here a live operation continues with `onStart` fired and a terminal owed, the visual frozen on one axis, and `NaN` in every geometry object and in the pinned target — the same composition E-05's reason names. **D-77's rule points the same way**: a consumer scalar's domain belongs to the seam that consumes it, and this one is consumed by `action.prepare`. **The channel differs from E-05's deliberately, and the difference is the blast radius, not the principle.** `home`'s value can only be refused by failing the seam that produced it, so it is classified and the drop stands; `moveTo`'s can be refused **before it is written at all**, so refusing it costs the operation nothing — and classifying it would end a live drag over a consumer's arithmetic, which is a worse answer than the poisoning it replaces. Both validate at the seam that consumes the point, and both leave the operation standing. | 07 §Validation's silence on `moveTo` |

**CE1-10 and CE1-11 are recorded as Checkpoint E evidence and change nothing.** CE1-10 measures the shared composition vocabulary and finds it thin: `realm` has one installer reader, `report` has none — it is read by the two **assemblers** — and `root` has no reader anywhere and **denotes a different element per tier**, the collection root for the sortable and the dragged item for free drag. That is evidence _against_ widening F-64, not for it, and the one thing genuinely owed is a sentence documenting `root`'s per-tier meaning — a doc fix, not a redesign. **That sentence is now written, in [03](03-feature-composition.md) §A fragment is a plain declarative partial config, on `FeatureContext` itself**: the sortable's `root` is the **collection root** and free drag's is the **dragged item**, one role and two referents. The third Checkpoint E pass found the deliverable still outstanding four days after the disposition recorded it as the only thing owed, which is the ordinary fate of an obligation written in prose and booked to nothing; it is discharged **normatively** here rather than left to a source comment, and `shared/composition.ts` mirroring it is follow-through with nothing left to decide. CE1-11 answers carried item 5 with a count instead of an argument: after D-74 the vocabulary is behavior-neutral in **name** everywhere, and **three of thirteen stages have a single behavior producer** — two the sortable's, one free drag's. Two behaviors is a small sample and a single-producer stage is not thereby a behavior-shaped stage; the item closes against the measurement rather than against a claim, and **no vocabulary changes**.

### The calling convention's second tier (D-92)

D-90 closed for free drag and the closure exposed the symmetric question it had assumed away.
